LADIES' LETTER.

ME DEAR NELLIE, The bright crude and pinks look garish, and hardly carry the stamp of good dressing upon them. A real rich red, on the other hand, is attractive, but after all the quieter tones look more chic. The dominant note of gold has intro- duced a cheerful element into our winter dress schemes, and with that it would be well to rest content, for allied with too brilliant colours it becomes vulgar at once. The gold roses and choux whieh are now lavished on our newest headgear are not nearly so alluring as those large flowers of velvet just tipped with gold. Coloured veils are also reappearing, and • are dotted with chenille which takes so-called vermicelli patterns over a very fine net background. A ginger brown veil worn all over a black tulle toque, on shich a couple of scarlet and black pompoms were poised, gave an impression of intense smartness to its wearer, whose hair was of the correct auburn hue, and whose clinging panne gown was wholly black. Her boa was brown, like her veil, and it was difficult to see where the one began and the other left off.

Ermine soils easily and is too conspicuous to be really suitable for day wear, except in the case of children, for whom it is, perhaps, the ideal fur. Next to thd very short coat in order of popularity comes the three-quarter length sacque with a "whole fox" boa collar instead of the more usual high one, and bell-shaped or bishop sleeves with cuffs matching the boa. Some of these coats are cut straight and square round the bottom and are double-breasted, but others have rounded fronts and are fastened with a single row of hooks or buttons. Sealskin and black fox, and caracul and sable, seem favourite combinations in this special type of coat.

- Change is ever welcome, if only as an experi- ment, and so it is quite on the cards that the new bon-bon tinted gloves will be liked, though it is ages since bright blue, pink, and purple handgear was deemed anything but vulgar. These gloves are intended to match in colour, and to be worn only with frieze tailor-made suits. For panne- cloth afternoon toilettes, black, grey, and the tans are much worn, and white Suede is also being seen again, ruinous though it is, in murky Decem- ber weather. No gloves are sold for ordinary day wear that have more than four buttons, and three is the popular number.

Although chains of all sorts are still used for the purpose, muff-guards of black moire ribbon with small gold ornaments or buckles at intervals ore newer and really more tasteful. The fashion- able coloured muff-linings, by the way, are now sold separately, all ready made up for slipping in the muff. Those of white satin or brocade with lace frills are smartest-looking, but they soil rather too quickly to be useful. Sets of buttons in ,pretty morocco cases are likely to find many buyers, so pretty, and in most instances so in- expensive, are they. Some set are of silver, cut and open-worked, while others are enamelled with tiny coloured blossoms forget-me-nots, daisies and jasmine. Belt buckles' are to be had in the same styles, but the flexible snake belt of gun metal with gilt stars is the very latest novelty. Fans are small, and nine out of 10 are of white gauze ornamented with silvery spangles, either used alone or surrounding insertions of lace, or small painted medallions. The sticks are of carved ivory, and every fan is a miracle of delicate workmanship. There is no decided novelty in purses or bags to note. The gilded or silver chain purse is still much in vogue, and is to be had in many sizes. So many belts of gold ribbon tissue are spoilt by not being properly mounted. It should be fitted on the figure, properly pinned and adjusted, and well boned and mounted on a foundation; then we shall get a well-shaped corselet belt. The com- bination of gold and chinchilla is an attractive one. I have seen a bolero of chinchilla completely covered with a design of bold scrolls and arabesques in half-inch wide gold braid, the fluffy fur standing up between the intricacies of the pattern. It seemed rather a :we of gilding the lity, but, after all, chincilla is somewhat of an insipid fur. The fiat has gone forth that the basque is to be added to the newest winter bodices, which, it seems, may pouch as they will, in front, at the back, and at the sides too, and be belted; but must have bob-tails quite short and pleated to take off the square look the Eton and bolero have for so long given to the figure at the back. Even in fur bob-tails are piquant.

Your loving JE'SNIE.

GIVERTZR APDO UGAL 1:S PATBNT S ELF-RAISING FLOUR

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The return from South Africa of the Duke, Prince Francis, and Prince Alexander of Teck, has been the signal for reje,cings at York House, St. James' and The Cottage, Sandringham. The Duchess of Y rk is devoted to her three brothers, and is seeing as much as possible of them before leaving for Australia. Prince Francis, who, if the Duchess makes any exception at all, is Her Royal Highness's favourite, has inherited no inconsider- able share of his father's good looks. Like all the mem- bers of the Royal Family, he never forgets a face, and always renews his acquaintance with anyone with whom he has had the most casual meeting. More roomy quarters have at length fallen to the lot of the Duke and Duchess of York, who will henceforth take up their residence, when in town, at Clarence House. The Duchess of Saxe-Coburg Gotha, whose dis- like of the Metropolis, and indeed, all things British, is well-known, has decided that she no longer needs the establishment, to the great delight of the Duchess of York, who now comes into possession thereof. York Cottage, Sandringham, like York House, St. James', the Duke and Duchess have also long since outgrown. It is just possi'ile, however, that the Duchess of York may even yet be granted the use of her old home, White Lodge (Richmond), it being very improbable indeed 'that the Empress Frederick will need it. Tbe betrothal of tbe youngest and only unmarried daughter of the Duchess of Saxe-Coburg Gotha, to the Hereditary Prince of Saxe-Weimar, is expected to be announced almost immediately. Her Royal Highness will not be seventeen until April next, but all her sisters were married before they were eighteen.and the Duchess is not likely to make an exception in the case of the Princess Beatrice. Tnis Princess—a very clever and charming girl—is a gcd-daughter and namesake of Prin- cess Henry of Battenberg. Mary Catherine. Countess of Derby, who died tbe other day, in ber 76'.h year, was the s-epmother of the Prime Minister. She was a danehter of the fifth Earl Do la Warr, and was married in 1847, as his second wife, to the Marquis of Salisbury, the father of the present Lord Salisbury. The Prime Minister was then Lord Robert Cecil. and as there was some little estrangement at the time between himself and his father, there was never any intimate friendship between Lord Salisbury and his stepmother. Two years after the late Marquis of Salis- bury died, in 1868, his widow married the Earl of Derby, who was afterwards the colleague in political life of the present Prime Minister. The late Earl of Derby died in 1893, and since his death Lady Derby lived in retire- ment at Holwood, near Bromley. She had five children by her first marriage, of whom four survive viz., Lords Arthur and Lionel Cecil, the Countess of Galloway, and Lady Margaret Cecil, who are, of course, the step- brothers and sister of Lord Salisbury. The Earl of Derby takes his title from West Derby in Lancashire, and not from the County of ll.rbyshire in which the Earl has not an acre of land. There are of course,manv peers whose titles have no connection with locality. The Duke of Devonshire for instance owns land in more than a dc z.n counties in Englann and Ireland, but not one of his 200,000 acres is in Devon. Another illustration is afforded by the Duke of Fife,who, though a large landowner,has no territorial connection with the couety of Fife. Lady Warwick, and others who have interested the in- selves in providing agricultural instruction for wcmen, are to be congratulated upon the success of the College at Readingoa bleb has just celebrated its seemed anniver- sary. If only we had the same facilities for training. there is no reason why we shou d be behind the Flemish women, who, as a rule are splendid agriculturists. We have a large surplus population, and ample rcpe for work in the small cultures, if only there were bands, properly equipped to do it. In Belgium, there are hun- dreds of small technical schools scattered about the rural districts, giving gratuitous instruction to girls in dairy work, and practical agriculture. Then there is the great College of Heverle, which has about 750 pupils. Most of these are farmers' dauehtera who go to Reverie to finish their education and where the daily life alternates between theoretical work in the class- rooms, and practical work in the farrrs and gardeaq. The total fees do not exceed £l2 a year—which is only a fraction of what they are at Reading. But having made a start, it may be hoped that the movement for training women in agiisultural pursuits will be ex- tended in this country, and it is a subject which might well be taken up with advantage, by the County Councils.

This is the season of appeals for many charitable pur- poses at home, but there are also one or two relatinz to South Africa. which claim attention. One is that of the South African Loyal Women's Guild, which is ask- ing for help in providing a fund, and the necessary attention for maintaining and beautifying the graves of our fallen soldiers in South Africa. The (alter is that of the South African Women and Children Distress Fund, the object being to feed, clothe, and shelter women and children, of any nationality who have been rendered destitute by the war. Mr. Chamberlain has expressed his belief that the Boers have destroyed as many farms and homesteads, as tbe British, and if this is so, there must be many of our own country people who are in need of help. Moreover, in every case that this is offered to the Dutch families, it will help to soften bitter feel- ings, and begin the work of reconciliation, which is the one hope for the future well-being of the annexed Colonies.

In glancing over the Christmas cards for the year, one cannot but be struck with the extraordinary inventive- ness of the designers. One firm issues na fewer than 1,200 entirely new sets of cards requiring at least 3,000 separate designs, a really wonderful diversity. Even more beautiful than the csrds however, are the calendars, some of which are veritable works of art. Singularly enough among all the diversity, there is a total absence of war subjects and references, possibly from tha feeling, that matters martial are not in consonance with the season whieh brings peace on earth, goodwill to all man- kind, but the reason more probably has in the fact that all designs are prepared so many months in advance, that topical events could not be brought up-tr.-late. Parisian leaders of fashion would seem to be making a pot-pourri of all the styles which bay^ flourished during the expiring century for a Louis XV. coat, a , Mar- quise'hat, a Watteau pleat, or an Empire robe, are seen with the most fin-de-sleeks styles. But so clever is the adaptation, that there is nothing incongruous in the combination, a sure indication that taste is improving. That interesting collection by at the Palais des Costumes, is to be preserved, and the original scheme will be enlarged upon and added to, as time goes on. This way lies wisdom, for there was no more perfect exhibi`.; in the wonderful Exposition.

For smart winter fur ccats, caracul with revers of er- mine or sable is the fur par excellence. It is as supple and adaptable as velvet, and is not in the least clumsy. So much of the success of a fur coat depends on tha cut and the figure of the wearer, but with canon!, success is assured. The fur blouse is also very becoming in cara- cul, and when trhomed with gold galon and ornamented with large buttons, is a confection to be coveted, and acquired if possible. One of the nrvelties fol this year is the beautiful j.we!lery, calird in Paris. "Nouvesu Art." It is the most notable departure in really :esthetic ornarrenta tion, since the time of Benvenuto Cellini. In pendants and clasps it is especially effective and its subdurd rich coloring is most varied. It is haowever somewhat xper - sive. With the revival of ol(-!ashioned jewellery, the cameo, co beloved of our great aunts, is coming into favour again. The small stones make combs or dides to fasten the loose hairs at the back of the head, and when set with prarls or diamonds, look well. The larger cameos are used to fa eon boas and cape., in fact there, seems to be a variety of uses to which th's pretty orna: ment may be assigned. _ _ _

White la stifi the vicar for quite tiny but when a colour is permitted this is usually of a dull werigwopd tint of blue, than which nothing is more bec.ming. In fur, white Thibet stands first favourite for the rursery f cik, partly no dcubt cn account of its softncss, and partly on accouel of tre delightful habit it has of returning from a jcurney to the cleavers. lonking like unto new. Patty hocks are being Extensively built of

white Oriental satin, which is an excellent fabric for this purpose. It is pretty and suitable. and is not easily torn or creased. The best built frocks are quite straight and simple, smocking, and rows of insertion being the only form of trimming deemed necess.ry or desirable.

" MADGE."

STREET BETTING. --- Prosecutions at Warrington.

Heavy Penalties.

Two young men named williem Green, jun.. of 19, Greenell-street, and Thomas Hackett. of 11, Earle-street, appeared before the Mayor and other magistrates at the Warrington Police Court on Monday, on a charge of frequenting Winwick-etreet for the purpose of betting with other persons. contrary to the bylaws on the 4th, sth, 6th and 7th of December. The Town Clerk conducted the prosecution on behalf of the police and explained that these were the first cases under the bylaws, which had been brought before the court. Although there bad been many complaints from the Bench and other person. about the p•evalence of the great offence of betting in Warringtnn, the difficulty had been to detect offenders. The men were as crafty as foxes, and all kinds of expedients were used to avoid detection. It was with great diffitulty the police had been able to get the present case. It was no goo making complaints, unless the police were supported in the matter, and if the present csees were proved he asked that the Bench would assist the police by imposing the full penalty. The mischief wrought in the town by neop:e who systematically carried on betting was untold, and he had beard on good authority that the wives ot some men actually complained that they were unable to buy bread because their husbands spent their money in betting. Detective Albert Me.cock was then called and gave evidence as to peeing the defendants taking bets on each of the dates in question. From instructions he received he proceeded to Winwick—street near the Central Station and watched the defendants between 12 noon and two o'clock on the 4:h inst. Re saw the firer, narneel defen- dant receive eleven bets. At 2-50 he eaw him receive a bet from another man near the Central Station steps. He saw Hackett receive three bets the same day on the footpath at the cerner nf Crown-sereet and the approach to the Central Station. The toin half of the hundred pound note, and another whole one, would be handed her at the porter's lodge in case she presented herself there with Miss Ward as a companion. By the time that Lord Carstairs and his assistant thoroughly understood one another, it was growing dark. Still there was time to go in a cab to the lodging-house kept by Drake's old servants, and, having parted from her employer, she betook herself there at once. •

Every room in the neat little house was shown her by the unsuspecting hostess, and to Miss Monstance's apparently innocent inquiry if there were "any young lady lodgers" she might make friends with if she came, the woman reluctantly replied in the negative. There had been a very sweet young lady only a few days ago, but she had been called suddenly away, on Monday, a week since; and the only other lodgers at present were an elderly married couple just come in. Monday, a week ago That gave the needed clue. Sara Ward, then, had only remained a single night. Miss Monstance dared not ask ques- tions concerning the episode lest the woman's suspicions should be aroused; but she saw by (.!-- • speaker's face that the lodger's departure had boa: a matter for regret. "Ran away, very likely," thought the amateur detective. "In that case, my only chance is to inquire in the neighbourhood as carefully as may be, and find out if anyone saw her sneaking off. The landlady may have her own reasons for not mikin;, too much fuss about her lodger's absence, and I'd have the advantage there." Miss Monstance departed, remarking that she liked the rooms, and would perhaps return next morning to engage one. This, of course, she had no intention of doing; but she did come back to the neighbourhood the following morning, after she had again arranged for it 'substitute in the office. Try as she might, however, she could learn nothing. "Sara Ward" might as well have disappeared into space on leaving the house where for one night she had been a lodger. Still, the promise of two hundred pounds dazzled the girl frem the Addressing Agency. Undaunted by one failure, she said doggedly to herself that she would return to the hunt next day. Patience is usually rewarded, and the patience of" Miss Monstance was no exception to the rule. On the morning of the day which, according to the papers, was appointed for the funeral of Sybil Wade's father. at Carstairs House, luck, in the shape of a small boy playing marbles, came her way. He was stopping at home from school (he assured the sharp-eyed young woman, who gave him a bright new sixpence to open the conversatiou? because of "toothache." He often had it, and WU kept out of school in consequence (this with a sly twinkle of the eye). Yes, he had suffered badly on the Monday a week ago—he quite remembered that. He had been rolling his hoop that morning. He recollected seeing a fair girl, with a lot of yellow hair, walking very fast. She had come round the corner of his street in a tremendous hurry, and he had trundled his hoop after her, to see what she would do, because she looked so queer. He hadn't spoken to anyone about it since, because, after all, it had turned out to be of no importance, and, besides, he'd been to school quite regularly every day since then till now. She'd only walked on till she saw a growler, had hailed the driver, and got in. The driver was a nice old chap, named Toni Atkins. He came that way sometimes, and had been known to "give a feller" a ride on the seat beside him. He lived in Rosemary-mews. Yes, the boy was sure of that. And so at last Miss Monstance went on, having got extremely good value for her sixpence. She had a long distance to walk before she reached Rosemary-mews; but she did finally reach it, and of an old woman who was a dweller there she readily learnt that the "Atkins had a young lady lodger, who was tykin' Fan Atkins's plice in a restaurant called the Busy Bee, somewheres along the Fulham-road." Miss Monstance could scarcely refrain from clapping her hands with joy. She seemed to see the other half joined on to her torn hundred- pound note; and she began to feel more excited and happy than she had ever felt in her monotonous life.

HARRIS'S, 28-30, BERRY STREET, LIVERPOOL.

ASSESSMENTS. ---- " The Swan Inn," Newton.

Mrs. Naylor, on behalf of John Naylor, landlord of the " Swan inn," Newton Common, appealed at War- rington on Friday, against the assessment of the premises, which was £9O gross and £76,103 rateable. It was stated tnat the assessment had been increased by £3O groas. The appellant; stated in writing that the "Swan Inn "was essentially a summer house and was principally dependent upon that season. There had been extensive structural alterations, without a correspond- ing increase in the business ot the house, which also suffered through the deprivation of the races. Mrs. Naylor stated that the rent of the home was £5O a year.

In reply to Mr. Billinge she said that although from £2,000 to £3.00 bad been spent on improvements there had been no increase of business. They sold %bout eight barrels weekly in summer but hardly any in winter. Being isolated they had not the same facilities as the town homes. When Newton races were held they used to make enough out of them to pay the rent.

The Asiseesment Committee -declined to alter the assessment.

Mrs Naylor said her husband would have to give up the house as they could nor make it pay on the present conditions.

"The Railway Hotel," Earlestown.

Mr. George Appleton appealed against the increased assessment of the "iltailway Hotel," Earlestown, which was £l6O gross and £136 rateable. It was stated that the rent was £lOO and the appellant urged that the business done, did not warrant the increase of £lO on the assessment, which had been made. The Chairman said the committee could not see their way to uphold the appeal.

CHAPTER XXIV.

AN OLD ENEMY AGAIN.

From Lord Carstairs's point of view, the conversa- tion passed off satisfactorily. Whatew:r Drake suspected regarding the disappearance of Ifester, he asked no questions, as aprar:tntly Hester had ,been true to Sybil's interests, and refrained from betraying her whereabouts to Carstairs. As for the notice in the papers which had just appeared, he naturally had some questions to ask, but he prudently affected to be satisfied with the answers he received, and kept his thoughts to himself for the present. Carstairs therefore got away from his ,quondam friend and confidant without having said anything;;which he would have to regret later; and, leaving the impression that he still kept his old faith in Drake, he finally departed.

What he said was, that he was going to do some- thing which he had neglected—buy a wedding present for Gladys. What he did, was to go to the nearest post-office and consult a directory. lie found Robertson's Eclair Addressing Agency with- out difficulty, and drove there at once in a hansom. ,He was received by Miss Monstance, Sybil's old enemy, who had glared jealously from narrow grey eyes over her typewriting machine on the first day of Sybil's appearance at the agency. Carstairs did not. wish to be known, as, should he give his name, the fact that he had called and made inquiries might reach Beauchamp Drake. He represented himself as a Mr. Smith, in business in the West End of London, who wanted to inquire the prices at which the agency worked. He had been recommended to Mr. Robertson's Agency, he said, by a young lady he had met in a business way; he rather thought she was employed in the establishment. At the moment he had forgotten her name; but she was quite a young girl, not more than seventeen, small and slender, with great quantities of curly, pale yellow hair, eyebrows many shades darker, and a very pink and white complexion. Was there a lady answering that description working in the office? The smile of welcome with which Miss Mon- stance had greeted a new customer, in the absence of Mr. Kittredge (who had a cold, and had stopped at home that day) gradually faded from her sharp visage, which clouded sulkily over. No, there was no lady answering that description, she replied, bluntly. Had there been such a one? If MiEs Monstance would endeavour to recollect, she might expect to have it—er—made worth her while. The lady in question had neglected to return some valuable manuscripts she had been copying, and he was anxious, if possible, to learn her address. When Miss Monstance discovered that this handsome though peculiar-looking young man ("quite a swell," as she put it to herself), was not a possible lover for the girl she had hated, her objections to telling what she knew vanished into thin air. She informed the gentleman that a Miss Sara Ward—or a young person calling herself Sara Ward—who was of the type lie described, had been in the office, but had left, without a word of warn- ing, a week ago. A friend of Miss Ward's—Mary Carter, a gloomy, dark girl, with big black eyes, had disappeared on the same day; but as the em- ployes did that sort of thing from time to time, and these girls were of no importance, so far as she knew, no inquiries had been made. However, there was a friend of the proprietor's, a solicitor, who had taken a fancy to Miss Ward, and he might know something. Miss Ward had looked like a flirt _ _ _

) "My MSS. were very -,-.:1117;1)1e," said Carstairs, in a reflective tone. "I eon 't wish to apply to the police in this case "—which was true, indeed, for reasons he was not likely to mention—"but I'd give anyone a hundred pounds who would show me exactly where to put my hand on the girl." Miss Monstance's sallow face flushed deep crim- son. If she had a hundred pounds she could do many things—best of all, she could marry Mr. ,Kittredge, and they would set up in business for themselves. Besides, she would have been pleased to do anything which might injure "Sara Ward," without payment. "Give me twenty-four hor le exclaimed, '‘and I'll find her for you."

CHAPTER XXV.

TIIE PATIENCE OP 21ISS MONSTANCE.

, When the bargain had been struck between Lord Carstairs and Miss Monstance, the first thing that the astute young woman did was carefully to think 'matters over. In ten minutes she had formed a plan of action. She knew where "Sara Ward" and "Mary Carter" had lived, and went to Thameston-street. Their room was still unoccupied, and she, posing as an inquiring friend of the absent girls, asked to be shewn up there, in the hope that she might come across some clue to their whereabouts. In a cheap waste-paper basket she spied a number of papers which had not been thrown out, and a crumpled note from Mr. Robertson told her that Sara Ward had been doing some work for the pro- prietor of the agency. , Miss Monstance ha l in her all the ferretty instincts of the born detective. Besides, she was now on her mettle, playing for what seemed to her a great stake. She discovered, through questioning the landlady at Tharneston-street, that her two young lodgers had gone out on a Sunday afternoon, carrying with them some papers, and had never afterwards returned.

! "They went to Mr. Robertson's, I'll bet V Miss Monstance inelegantly assured herself. Having left the office in charge of a responsible person, she was free to pursue her investigations, and proceeded straight to Craven-street; for, though she had never been to Mr. Robertson's flat, she knew the house by sight. She did not ring the bell which would produce his servant in response to a summons, but rang for the janitor. A tip, paid from Miss Monsta.nce's savings, sharpened his memory, and he. presently recalled the fact that be had seen such a girl as Miss Monstance described. She had come, stayed a few minutes, and departed in the company of a gentleman who occasionally visited Mr. Robertson—a well-known solicitor, Mr. Beauchamp Drake. More than this the janitor could not tell; but, hoping that she had already earned her hundred pound's, Miss Monstance took a cab to a quiet restaurant in the Haymarket, where Carstairs had told her she might, if she had anything to com- municate, ask for, and find "Mr. Smith" between certain hours in the afternoon.

Carstairs was there to keep the appointment, and anxious for new. His face flushed, and he tightened his lips when he heard that which made him more certain than ever of Bcauchamp Drake's treachery • but he was equally certain that, what- ever had fiappened a week ago, Drake had somehow lost touch with Sybil now. If he had not—if ho had been able to mould the girl's actions according to his will—he would have married her by this time, crying: "Checkmate !" to Lord Carstairs's plans. Drake's manner had e;pressed concealed anxiety when he bad spiten of the death notice which Carstairs had imu,ried in the papers, and from that the other judg;,,i that his false friend had failed in his own schemes.

"The girl wouldn't him, and most likely she's contrived to slip bet ween his fingers as she did with me, though she has no Hester to help her now," • Carstairs said to himself. He knew of the lodging-house kept by the man and woman who had once been in Drake's service, for Drake had at one time proposed that Carstairs should have certain dealings with them before the old house by the river had been definitely decided upon as the scene of action. Now Carstairs guessed that the solicitor had had a thouOit for his own advantage even then ; and, in remembering the lodging-house keepers, he suspected that Drake tllight in this instance have taken Sybil there. He was particularly anxious to keep his dis- coveries and investigations from Drake's knowledge, partly from a cowardly fear that Drake would instantly seek revenge, partly lest he should be thwarted at the outset ; and he congratula;ed him- self on having so clever an assistant as this young woman from the Addressing Agency. Her spiteful enmity against Sybil had been betrayed by word and look Oierefore, she could be relied upon for a personal motive even deeper than cupidity; and in pursuing the case still ftirther, Lord Carstairs intended to screen himself behind Miss Monstance.

CHAPTER XXVI.

THE FACE OF THE DEAD.

Jack Churchill ought to have considered himself one of the most fortunate men in England, if not the world. Rich, good-looking, popular, young, and engaged to marry a great beauty—for whose sake more than one man had broken his heart—it must have appeared to an outsider that he had little left to wish for. Yet he was unutterably miserable, and in the last few weeks had grown old in feeling that there was nothing to make life worth living. The more he saw of Gladys Wade, the more dis- illusioned he became. He had never loved her, but he had chivalrously credited her with many good qualities which he saw, now that the girl had been literally forced upon him, she did not possess. Literally, her face was her fortune. Heart she. had none, of mind only enough to scheme success- fully against a rival, or "assume a virtue when she had it not." Without her beauty she would have been bankrupt, indeed ; and poor jack Churchill, whose quixotic generosity had led him into his present position, had reached a pass when, not only had Gladys Wades classic featm-es lost their charm, but become well-nigh repulsive. The news of Sybil Wade's death, which came to his ears through Lady Carstairs before it went in tics the papers, was not only, a shock but a revelatic to Jack. When Sybil lived, he had not dared tti; let himself realise that, though seen only once, she was all the world to him. But when he was told that she was dead his heart spoke, and told him the truth. He had hardly seemed to have any part in arranging the date for his forthcoming marriage; and if it could have been postponed for any other reason on earth save this one, he would have been inestimably relie7ett. But Sybil—beautiful little wistful-eyed Sybil -- v,-,ts dead ! Nothing else mattered since that was so. Lady Carstairs said that the proper thing would be to put off the wedding for a few days till after the funeral. Nobody who knew anything of the circumstances could expect more, as really poor little Sybil had been almost a stranger, and they had already been far kinder to her than they were actually calit.•:.,cl upon to be. Jack listened dazedly, his only e.,;,,ecious wish being to get away front her cold eyes,. ad Gladys's dark, questioning gaze, fixed upon ;4!;,s paling face. Sybil wet! kad! He thought of nothing else that day, and 4,• not sleep that night I,)r the agony of thinkiw Ilat death had stolen the sweetness he had loved, rte was so vounz. If only things had

'I HE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, DECEMBER 14, 1900.

PROPOSIM A.CCOMSIODATION.

Drawing Offices, 10 places. Apparatus Store, Mechanics' laboiatory. Woodworking shop with benches for 24 boys. Plumbing workshop and metallurgy laboratory, one storey building). DOM EST IC DEPART lENT. PROPOSED ACCOMMODATION. Cookery room to accommodate 54 for lecture or 36 for practice. Laundry for 14 at practice. Dressmaking room. We have already pointed out that the Com- mercial and Continuation classes will not be accom- modated in the new Technical School. At a lacer date it is intended to build behind the proposed building and provide the necessary accommodation with an entrance from Museum-itreet. It has also been decided to postpone the consideration of pre- viding accommodation for the pupil Teachers Centre and the establishment of a Higher tirade Mixed School. These will be taken into con- sideration at a later date, Another scheme which has been postponed is the prevision of an assembly hall. In the course of his report on the proposed new Technical School Mr. Nladeley stated that the work of the institute had been considerably hampered in the past by the want of a mederate-sized aentably hall, in which prize distributions, general lectures, and similar functions might be carried on. The committee, however, found it impossible to include this in the present building, but it is to be hoped that in the near future, the Technical Instruction Committee will be able to meet all the requirements for techni- zal education in this busy manufacturing town where such education is essential if we are to hold our own in the industrial world of to-day.

The accpuuts were approved on the motion of Mr. VBRE DAVIE 9 secomiel by Mr VmsEr. LAST YEAR'S SHOW-REPORT BY THE SECRETARY.

The SECRETARY read the annual report which statei that the eleventh annual show was again held in Walton Park by the kind invitation of the President, air Gilbert Greenall, on Thursday, 9:h August, 1900. The Mayor of Warrington (Mr. Henry Roberts) was the vice-presa dent for the year and the society are indebted to him for the support he has always given the society since its inauguration both as a subscriber and an exhibitor. Apart from the bad state of the weather the show was a success. There was a " record " entry in most classes and the judges spoke very highly of the exhibits. ThR jumper class, which was a special feature of this year's show, had not a large entry, there bsiag other shows about the same time, having an older repueation for this particular class. The cattle chases are not sufficiently supported, especially in a district so well known for its dairies ; but it may net be properly understood by owners of cattle and tenant farmers that with the exct Wien of Use class for pedigree bull no particular breed is necessary, and pedigrees are not required, the idea of the society being to offer prizes for the best dairy cattle. As regards the classes, for shires it would be noticed that there were very few barren mares, and tee largest class was for mares with foal a': foot, which in itself was most satisfactory. The Shire Horse Society gave their add medal, and silver medals were given by the Hunters Irnprov -- ment Society, and the Hackney Horse Scciety. This year the silver challenge cup given by Sir Gilbert Greenall for the best shire entry in mares and fillies, 'filly foals and colt foals, leaves the socie,y, the winner having won it outright. An epee draught gelding class was well supported, there being 13 entries. and together ' with the draught gelding shown in the district class made a good display, and the withdrawal of the conditions as to the entries in these classes having to be eligible for lentry in the Shire Stud Book created keener competition as the exhibits were not limited to shires. The president has long been ef opinion that the society ought t enlarge its work, and last year submitted a scheme for the con- sideration of the members. It is for them to say whether this scheme has pmved satisfactory. Sir Gilbert Greenall spared no pains to make this year's show successful. He laid an entirely new ring at great cost, and when the comm ttee Ir!sitatl:l to flat such an enlarged prize schedule on account of the risk in finances he cffered to guarantee all lons. In consequence of the bad weather there naturally is a deficit in the accounts, but Sir Gilbert, in fulfilment of his prormise, enabled she society to meet all its liabilities. An additional item of expeaditure was the new railing round tee ring. Mr R A Naylor, of Warrington, again gave his support to the wark of the society and only charged a nominal sum for a very strong and useful fence 'file cot:emit:me earnestly invite the members of the Society to co-operate with them for the successful work- ing of the society, not only in remitting their subscrip- tions early but endeavoering to obtain additional members and bringing forward to the notice of the neighbourhood generally the advantages derived from supporting a society of the kind, A resolution passed at, a meeting of the committee to the effect that all exhibitors wishing to take the advantage of the lesser entry fees by beceming members of the society should pay two years' subscription was, after due considsra- tion, struck out of the minutes, but it was decided to defer the matter for final settlement by the members of the society at the annual meeting in December. Before closing the report the committee with to take this opportunity of thanking the hon.veterinary surgeons, Nlr Joseph Welsby, of West Derby, near Liverpool, and Mr Hugh Ferguson, of Warringtorefor their continued help. The stewards also deserve the greatest praise for the excellent way in which they carried through their arduous duties, the judges again congratulating the society on having thee a band of energetic workers. It is almost unneeessary to repeat that a great atmouet of the success of a show of the kind depends upon the manner and promptness in whica the stewards conduct their share of the work. The committee have cs.refaily considered the rules of the society, which they submit, in a reduced form, for the approval ana adoption by the members, and they also wish to suggest for the con- sideration of the members at the annual meeting, that a recommendation be made by thin to the General Com- mittee to adopt the prize schedule of the last show with a few slight alteratians, which will be more fully con- sidered by the Executive Committee.

Interesting Proceedings.

At the invitation of the directors of the Warrington British Workmen's Public-hou,se Company, Limited, a small company assembled in the drawing room at the IVinmarleigh Cato, Warrington, on Wednesday afternoon to attend a pressmtation to the secretary of th 3 Company (Alderman Henry Rob?.rts, J.P., Mayor of Warrington), Light itfrestiments were handed round from four o'clock to five. Thero were present :—The Mayor and Mayoress and Miss Roberts, Mr and Mrs Itatv rt Garnett, A!th•rman

Shaw tireen,.Councillor and Mrs Wiliidna Roberts, Coun- cillor a(ul Mrs Brough, Mr and Mrs Jackson Holmes, Mr and Mrs T Birchall, Mrs Yonge, Mr Jatin Fairhurst (borough treasurer), Mr and Mrs I,o(4:land, Mr and Mrs J W Hayward, Mr Isaac Hayward (WakAiela), Mr Francis Brown, Mr ard Mrs John Critchiev, Mr and Mrs Taylor (Macclesfield). Mr E A Connor, Mrs Lightburn, Mr and Mrs Thomas Hawthorn, aLd Mr and Mrs Joseph Podmore.

The formal proceedings were presided over by Mr J W Hayward, and at the outset he read letters of apology for noo-atteudsace from the Rev John Yonge, Mr E J Guest, Mr arid Mrs Jona Fairclough, ilr Z ba Armitage, and Mr William Uns worth (Latchford). The Rev John Yonge wrote " December Bth, 1900. Dear Sie,.—l regret that an important engagement in Londom will prevent my being present on Monday at the presenta- ma to Alderman Roberts. I shall be glad if you, will: kindly explain my unwilling absence on so interesting and gratifiying an occasion. Faithfully yours Jorns YONGI3. C Jackson Eloleoes Eig " Proceeding he said that four years ao the Britian Workman Public-House Company celenrated its cemiog of age, when the direc- tors and secretary marked the occasion by presenting to teeir then chairman the late Mr Thomas Morris) a token ot appreciation of his valued services to the company and Les generous and kindly spirit to those associated with him. That day they celebrated their 25th anniver- sary or their silver vr edding ? (Hear, hear.) Directors and shareholders had alike combined to rec.,geiz, the 25 years' faithful service of their Secretary. As actions spoke more effectively than words they were wishful that what they did that day might be a sincere expres- sion of their appreciation of his service, worth, and abit- ity• (Hear, hear.) Be would now ask one of the city fathers, Mr Robert Garnett who had been associated with the company from its formation to make the pre- sentation to their Secretary, his Worship the Mayor. (Applause.)

• Mt' ROBERT GARNETT said he did not know that the British Workman Public-house Company could have been under better guidance than it had been since its formation ; he had watched it through the various years, and he was somewhat surprised to find that it had been in existence for 25 years. He remembered taking an interest in the houses in Liverpool when the late Rev. Chance Garrett:. was to the front, and he thought it was a very hopeful sign when their Warrington friends took the matter up aud established similar public-houses, where men could have some:bing to eat and drink and not be expected to spend their money in something 'which was diametrically opposed to their own intere-its. (Hear, hear ) With Alderman Roberts as secretary of the Company and with his brother, Mr. William Roberto also counected wita it, it was sure to succe el. The property was a large and valuable one, and was worth vastly more than it cost, and the company was now in a good position. Of Alderman Roberts he could not say too much. He felt proud of him, and he was proud of him as Mayor. (Hear, hear.) He had known every Mayor since the incorporation of the borough—some of them pretty well. He (Mr. Garnett) never took a great interest in politics, and he would not go into the Town Council, not because be did not like the work hut be- cause he would not go in on party lines. Be contended that the men they wanted were men with good square heads on, with time to devote to the duties ot the orfice, and men should not be elected because they were Whigs, Tories, or Radicals. In the Mayor they had a man whom they could trust. (Hear, hear.) He had not got his price. (Hear, hear.) He( Mr. Gar- nett) esteemed Alderman Ronerts for his integrity and high priuciples, and he could say the same of hie brothers. He wished they were a bigger family, as they were • good sort. (Hear, hear, and laughter.) Their lather was a good man and he knew and esteemed him and he was sorry that his life had been cut off at a corn- peratively early age. He was a most estimable man, and if he could only know what was taking place—no doubt he did—be would feel that honour was lasing done to the town by his sons. (Hear, hear.) He had much pleasure in presenting the Mayor w.th a handsome silver tea and coffee tray. He s'aculd never expect to see it at their uncle's—(laughter)—for he was quite sure A!cler- man Roberts would cherish it. (App!a use).

, . The tray, which was chaste in 'design, had been supplied by Messrs. Eu,tance nod Com- pany. It bore the following inscription :—" Pre- sented by the Directors of the Warrington British Workman Public-house Company, Limited, to Alderman Henry Roberts, J.P- , of Warrington, in recognition of 25 years.' taitlitul services as Secretary of the Comp toy. Dated December 10th, 1900 " Mr. JACKSON HOLMES (chairman of the company) next addre sod the meeting, and coniVlirnented Mr. Hayward upon the manner in which he had made the arrange- ments for the presentation ar.d said it was owing to the sbrewiness of Mr. Roberts that they now po:ses-eci that handsome piece of property at the bottom of lEl)l'3m:tat- ket-street.(Hear,hear.) If it haa no: peen for the manner in which he had coesisteotly and persistently advocated its purchase the company would not have been in a posi- tion to pay the handsome dividend it now did. But they did not make their profits on the goods they sold,as they gave such excellent value for everything. The profits from the sales were exceedingly small, and their income was largely augmented by the fact that they were their own landlords. It gave him very great pleasure to be. present on that occasion, and he was pleased that Mro Garnett had been able to make such a presentation t the Mayor. (Hear, hear ) Mr. T BIRCHALL said it gave him very great pleasure indeed to be present and help to do honour to a man, whom he believed from his heart was worthy of honour. (Hear, hear.) Alderman Roberts was a man who bad grown up amongst them. He had known the Mayor from his childhood. Unfortunately, like Mr. Garnett, he had known every M tyor of Warrington, and many of the inhabitants who were now to the front, since they were children, and he thought it was a credit to War- rington that most of its public men had risen from com- paratively poor positions. It showed that they had men of sterling worth and ability amongst them, men fitted to take their part in the battle of rife in a town sech as .their own. They had reason ;to complain of many of the public citizmis in Warrington. They had seen them make their fortunes here, and then quietly slide out of the town and decline to take up those duties which men who had obtained wealth an( leisure ought to perform. They went to their churches and chapels, and perhaps some of them repeated their creeds, but there were some plain things they frequently overlooked, and one was this " To wham much is given, from him much shall be re- quired." (Hear, hear). Ile was afraid that some of tneir wealthier men had not heeded that lesson. But they were fortunate in the class of men—of whom their worthy Mayor was one of the best—who filled the civic chair. They were men of ability who bad made their way in the world and could stand up to the front with anybody, and had taken their full share in the municipal lite of the borough. But he bad never been so proud of any Mayor as he had been of the present occupant of the chair. He was a Warringtonian born and bred, and he was a man of whom they were proud. If he went to any place at a distance he could represent them with credit ; that was not what they could have timid of all their Mayors so far as ability to do anything in ttie way of putting their case before the community was con- cerned. They weep proud to have that opportunity of showing their good-will to Alderman Roberts who had PO many claims to their respect, and they were proud t do him honour. (Hear, hear, and applause). Mr. JOSEPH PODMORE also spoke and.gave come inter- esting reminiscences of the early history of the company, with special reference to the building in Horsemarket- street, and contended that they owed mush of their prosperity to the ability of their worthy secretary. Ho had always felt cenfid-nee in anything he had put his hands to in the way of business. (Hear, hear). Mr. HAYWARD sai I ho trusted that the Mayor and his good wife would be able to drink tea from that tray until the Company c lehrated its goldee:wedding. The MAYOR, in acknowledging the gift, said it was not an easy thing to reply to matters so personal to him- self, andl'he found considerable difficulty in expressing what be should like to express to the Chairman and the other speakers and to all who had 60 generously sub- scribed to enable the company to naake such a handsome present to him. There wore two sides to the I work in connection with the company. One *as the purely financial side and the other the moral side. local Trades' Unionism.

Very little business of public interest was trans- acted at the monthly meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council, which was held in the Druids' Ilan, Sankey-street. on Tuesday evening, Mr. A C Binks presided: over a good attendance.— The Secretary read a letter from the Town Clerk, acknowledging the rec 4pt of a letter with refer- ence to municipal bowling greens from the Trades Council, which, he said, shoull be laid before the next meeting of the proper comnaittee.—Coun- cillor A Bennett, the Secretary of the Warrington Chamber of Commerce, wrote in repl y to a communi- cation from the Council, stating that it. had been decided to defer the question of the creation of a local Board of Conciliation until the annual meeting, which would nrobably be held on the last Monday in January. Could the Council arrange to send a deputation to the meeting, when the matter might belf ally discussed ?—The Chairman: We passed a resolution asking the Chamber to meet us. They ask us to appoint a deputation. I think three will be sufficient.— Mr. Battersby : It is -oecessary t ) have sufficient men to Se able to cope with men who can argue. On the last occasion on which they met the Chamber of Commerce, the deputation consis- ted of five members.—lt was decided that the deputotion consist of the Chairman and Messrs. !Plinston. Edwardson, Malkin, and Battersby.— Mr. Poleaore thought the Council should be I thoroughly prepared with the aims and objects they had in view in the matter of conciliation. It will be wise in the circumstances, to hold over that information. Mr. Horrocks thought the suggestion a wise one He would have seconded the proposition, but he did not know the meaning of the Conciliation Boar. or what the grounds were upon which they wished to be conciliatory to the Chamber. The decision of the deputation should be made known to the Trades Council before

the interview with the Chamber.—The Secretary replied that that would be done, as the Trades Council would meet again before the meeting of the Chamber was held.—Mr. Podmore's motion was carried.

St. Helens, Rainhill, and Prescot Tramway. On Monday morning the trial trip was run on the new section of the St. Helens anl district tram- ways, connecting the Thatto Heath end-with Rain- hilr and Prescot. It is expected that the extension lines will be open for traffic in about a fortnight. When this is done the electric cars will run direct from St Ht lens to Thatto Heath, and alongside the boundary walls of Rainbill Asylum to Rainhill, thence along the road past Whiqon Workhouse, and on to Prescot. where a junction has been effected with the present St Helens and Prescot lines. At stated distances along the route—the round journey from St Helens being some eight miles in length— passing places have been laid down for the service of cars running in the opposite direction. The new ei'ension will prove of great advantage to the residents along the line of route. - -

LAND PROBLEMS.

Paper by Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins. Land in its relations to English Social Life was the sutject of an excellent paper read by Mr W H J Jenkins 'before the members of the Warrington Literary and Philesophical Scciety on Monday evening. The Rev Morley Stevenson (president of the Society), in ceiling upore Mr Jeekins to read his paper said the subject was one ?rein he had no doubt would in his hand; be made very inte:eeting, though perhaps it did not appeal to ell of them in the way some subj cts might do. He did not know what their interest in laud might be. His own Was extremely small—(laughter)—and never likoly to be larger. Dealt with by Mr Jenkins he had no doubt the , Subject would invest itself with an interest which it did not perhaps practically bear for each one Of them at the preeerit moment. (Applause) Mr JENKINS very graphically portrayed the condition of the people of this country from the earliest times uatil the inception of the feudal system. He pointed ; out that side by side with the growth of the great mili- tary meendency, which exercised such a powerful ioflu- eine in the Councils of the nation, and contr )l over the occupancy of the land, the art of Agriculture had been ; making el w but steady proerese. This development, so heceesary for creating some regular method of providing a Constant source of subsistence' as a natural consr- 1. guenee, introduced and established the idea for a more Permanent property in the soil. The period, though it , ba3 irreverently been termed" the era of sealing wax and eheapskins," was not altogether a tad one for the tiller of the soil, the artisan, and the labourer. As the time went on, the land gradually, but surely. Passed into the hands of fewer owners, the position of the poorer classes and the peasantry becoming, worse in !ach succeeding generation, until we are t -Ad that " competitive rents and private ownership took the place of the old feudal system, and the landowners became 'emetically the masters of the country.'' The artisan and the labourer now held positions which, in com- Peeienn w,th their former lot, were little less than that 'of slaves. This might possibly be rather a strained view of the positiou. For many generations alter the feudal sestem had gained a firm held in this country there 'were many causes other than toe influence or the greed of ton govsrning claeses which tended to vest the land in cemparatively few hands. Commerce, though slowly growing, was not of sufficient importance to affect the Value of land. A reason is rather to be sougbt in the feet that, for generation after generation, the country Clad either been engaged in war with other countries or in the subduing of civil disaffections within her own borders—oftentimes in both at once. Proceeding to refer to the passing of the Settled Land Act he said the provisions of that Act had an important bearing on the land question. As the title of the Act states it was passed—" for facilitating tales, leases,and Other eiepositione of settled land, and for promoting the execution of hr3DlOVementS therein." The period was one in which land and law reform (the latter especially es affecting matters connected with the land) occupied h prominent place in the minds of legislators. It seems to be a point well worth consideration, whether the un- satisfactory position into which the land had clrifted,and the laws effecting it, were not occasioned by a fallaci- ous view of the landowners as to their rights of owner- Slip. There is a common, but erron-ous idea prevalent with regard to land. It is popularly assumed that the Proprietorship of land carries with it an absoute right of ownership, That this is not so every student of the question knows. It was largely due to a misconception of the true view of their position on the part of the landowners, which necessitated the passing of the teertled Lend Act of 1832. In fact, it was a measure paesed to save them from themselves. Many and varied are tho remedies suggested for a better adjus-ment of the land laws, and for bringing within the reach of every subject, easier methods of ac- quiring and enjoying land. There are in all probability none more drastic and comprehensive than those pro- peeled by the Lend Nationalization Society. The society advocates: 1, The taxation of and values ;2, the reform of the L -,nd Laws with the objet oft xten lir g the cte- tivation of the soil ; 3, tee right of public access to rivers, mountains, moors, and uncultivated land in general ; 4, an extension of the power of public bodies to acquire land for allotments, small holdings, town improvements, and other public purposes which may hereafter benefit the people; 5, the common riett (if every individual to rent a portion of the land ; 6, the acquisition of all mineral rights by the State ; 7, the abolition of private ownership,and the acquisition of the lan by the 5-ate ;8, compensation to dispossessed land- horde at 25 to 30 years' purchase of the net incomes ; 9, the reservatiou to the State of all future " unearned in- crements d of land values, and the allocation of the pro- fits from rents to the reduction of taxation. The society opposes : 1, all encroachments on rights of war, corn- merle, and roadside wastes, or footpaths ; 2, any measures tending to increase the number ot land- owners, or merely involving transfer of ownership. The Lecturer then reviewed the arguments in favour of this propaganda used by Mr W P Price-Heywood in "The Landless Man." Proceeding he said it would be itle to contend tbat there was in the land a possible e•eleeloernent which might prove a source of livelihood for thousands of earneet workers. Dealing with the (oration o; housing the poor. What is there, he asked, more painful to contemplate, than the badly-built Property, the squalid slums. the densely crowded courts atid tenement houses, with their unwholesome surround- Inge, which disgrace our cities and largo WWI:IS, and which are far more common in smaller country places ll.an the unobservant are aware of ? No thou4htful Inan can regard with other than painful feelings the aufferiegs of his poor fellow-creatures in this respect. The wretched housing of a large section of the toilers of this country has awakened the keenest sympathies of all sections of the ceminunity and of all political • rt'e7. It is a foremost queition of the day, demand- hie our deepest attention, and calling for the earliest Pcssible soaation. From what class was drawn " the thin red line" which has played so splendid a part in the history of our country? Who does not regard with f„''lings of pride the struggles in which our Army and -Navy have left FO glorious a record P There surely the Pcor man no less than the rich, has a heritage of which be may juNtly be proud. At the present moment is not the highest fseling of admiration evoked by the deeds of heroism urroap.ht by the rank and file of our forces in South Africa ? We know that, though it may not be ehe case with al!, yet too many of these men will return ti hcmes situated amid surroundings of squalor and vseetchedness which are a disgrace to the nation. What • indictment on this subject did the Rev J W Horslee- lay at the Newcastle meeting of the Cnurch Congress ! Us spoke from intimate knowledge of a congested dis- zrict in London. Of 4,575 cue-roomed houses in Spite:- rlolns. 1,400 were occupied by fram ten to eleven per- • ; inn one no less than seventeen persona occupied a °ingle room. Hie experience was that slum life stunted the groac th, that the stunted steal, that criminals and drunkards were made by over-crowded insanitary areas, and that bad landlords were largely responsible for --'airne. The ruffianly Hooliganism of which we read so much in the papers just now, whet is it but the natural outceme of such an environment ! To preach temper- e-tice, soberness, and chastity to dwellers in overcrowded and insanitary dens, without attempting to ameherate their condition, was, Mr Eloreley declared, more than 'futile ; It was an irreligious absurdity. turning to the remedies suggested, the lecturer dealt 10 detail with a scheme initiated by the C'-oPerarwd I:Vholesaie Society in the direction of co-operative agricul- eine and its success and referred to the increasing rise is, land values in and about large centres of popalation. Whereby landowners reaped enormous incomes in the form of ground or chief rents. It is urged, he said, by eotne that the rise in land values with no direct benefit aescruing to local taxation is now runt by the increase in d Path ciifjes,and that such contributions to the Imperial Exchequer fairly compensate for the loss which arises through the none taxation of land values. To the poor ea.ri thi?, argument offers hut little consolation ;i1 it be erne that these land values are not already taxed, and that if they were it would so greatly relieve local taxi- as te have an appreciable bearing upon the question f t'ae better housing of the poor and possibly lead to ~t 4 satisfactory solution, then, by all means, let them be taxed, A friend of his contended that land values are already taxed and that the present mode of assessment the poor rate amply provides the necessary machinery 17, .r the. incorporation. The overseers in preparing their asetten lists take into consideration the value of the • • riu or chief rents which may be reserved to the ;I,vener of tke land, and as that is the basis of all rating is held that the landed interests hear their full share ''of taxation. This is, however, a very debatable point.

The care of the poor and the protection and encourage- merit of the industrious workers in the community are amongst the most important questions for the considera- tion of the social reformer and the statesman. A crisis in a country's history often comes as a blessing in dis- guise. It,cent events have showe that when the oc !a- Mon arises both our rich men and our poor men can pull together with a will, one motive in common prompting and sustaining them, one goal their sole aim, the welfare of the Fatherland. May the same spirit pervade the thoughts and actuate the will of every member of the cornmugity when the time comes for the consideration of these social and other questions, in which the interests of the rich and the poor may seem to clash. The CHAIRMAN complimented Mr Jenkins upon his excellent paper. He was glad to hear what had been said in dispelling the fallacy that the man who had a little ground would make his fortune right away. It was his (the Chairman's) duty to manage a small dairy farm in connection with the Training College. It was worked principally with the object of providing them with pure milk. They had plenty of that, but he should a extremely sorry to have toi live on the profits of the term. (Laughter). He should have liked some infer mation regarding the depopulation of the rural districts. Was that really so to any great extent, and were they all going to be herded together in the large towns? Then there was the question of aericultural produce. There was a startlingly large increase in the amount of dairy produce iteported into the country every year. It was suggested to afford help to the farmers by granting a subsidy, but that was a very difficult question to settle satisfac only. Councillor A J JOLLEY proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Jenkins for his paper. Referring to the question of the taxation of land values, he said it seemed to him, to speak quite frankly, that C orlyle's epithet " clotted nonsense" might be justly used concerning a good deal that was said and written about the taxing and rating of ground values. That land was already taxed there could be no doubt. Some people said that the owner ought to be rated and taxed, distinguishing between the owner and the occupier. Where the owner was the oc- cupier it was so. His (the speaker's) point was they were rated:on:the full:annual value of the land,and people who wrote books or made speeches in which they assumed that by some change in the system the total revenue of the community would be increased were simply talking nonsense. The housing problem was a great problem, but many people attached too much importance to it. There was often misunderstanding and the state cf affairs was exaggerated. Tne rev, gentle- men who spoke at the Church Congress came under that category. If it were true that there were BO many single rooms in London containing so large a number of people there was something wrong with the work of the sanitary authorities in the district. This was not BO much a question of the houses as, to some extent, of the people themselves. There were people of so degraded a type that there was little hope for the success of meesures ta ken to improve their dwell- ings. That was rather hard, but it was true: He did not for a moment suggest that improved environment would not effect improvement, but it would not achieve complete reform. Mr. MAY, in seconding the vote, said the difficulties in the way cot land reform were the dead man's hand and the lawyer's jargon. The remedy was to make land a marketable commodity like any other article. The vote was heartily carried. Mr. JENKINS, in returning thanks, expressed the opinion that lack of sympathy between the farmer and the labourer had ca.used some depletion in the rural population. There was, however, more sympathy grow- ing up between them, and that would teed to keep a better class of labourers more attached to the land. The proceedings then terminated.

OUR ALMANACK ILLUS- TRATION.

The Proposed Technical School at Warrington.

In educational matters Warrington moves slowly. At a time when every neighbouring town bas erec- ted a handsome pile of buildings in which to house is technical instruction classes, Warrington is still considering a " scheme." It is true that events have now progressed so far that a plan of the pro- posed new technical school is obtainable, but a considerable period must elapse before we may con- gratulate ourselves upon having drawn into line with such near neighbours as St. Helens, Widnes and Runcorn in the accommodation made for techni- cal instruction. The classes already carried on by the fTechnical Instruction Committee are pro- vided with accommodation in a number of buildings in different parts of the town. The Science and Technical subjects for instance are accommodated at the Old Barracks, whilst the Commercial and Continuation classes are housed at the People's College. Then again, the plumbing class assembles at the Old Gas Works in Mersey- street; the classes in dressmaking and building con- struction are located at the Museum, and those in geometry and machine drawing at the School of Art. By means of a commix:icing Technical School building it is hoped to eventually draw all the classes to °tic centre. This will not be accomplished by the present scheme inasmuch as it is not pro- posed to find accommodation in the new building for the Commercial and Continuation classes. These will continue to be conducted at the People's College until such time as the committee is in a position to extend the new Technical School by the erection of further premises extending to Muse urn-street. Our illustration shows the front elevation of the proposed technical school.As will be seen it is sand- wiched between the Parr Hall on the one side and the Gymnasium on the other. The site is an admir- able one in many respects and the building will not only have a commanding frontage to Palmyra- square but its position at the foot of Springfield- street is such as to enable it to be seen from Sankey- , street. The building will be three storeys high and I will be built of pressed brick with stone dressings, and the style of architecture will be late English renaissance. Messrs William and Seger Owen are the architects and there is every reason to believe that the building will reflect credit upon them and be one which the people of Warrington can point to with pride. Ascending a flight of steps at the main entrance in Palmyra-Square the visitor will enter a wide corridar above the basement. The corridor will provide space for exhibiticri cases and convenience for hanging framed prints. In addition to the class- rooms for the students there will be an office, com- mittee room, students and reading rooms, store rooms and lavatories. The technical classes will divide themselves into three departments, namely, chemistry and physics department, mechanics and engineering department and the domestic depart- ment. The accommodation provided will be as follows :

CHEMISTRY AND paysics DEPARTMENT. übjecta now taught: Inorganic and Organic Chemistry, Iron and Bteel, Electricity, Theoretical Mechanics, Physiology. Hygiene.

CONSUMPTION THE RESULT OF

CARE LESSN ESS.

It is one of the greatest and most tragic pities in the world that nearly all cur failures and misfortunes— nearly all our unhappiness, and nearly all our ailments, are attributable to a want of care. " Doift care got hanged," is an old saying—and don't care as regards health, nearly always ends in a debilitated body and unsound lungs. A neglected cold may end in pneumonia or consumption, or permanent il!-bealth. Colds mu,t not bt. neglec:ed—it is folly—it is wicked—it is fatal to ignore them. One's own health,and that of one's family may pay the forfeit. Colds are infectious—hfluenzt is infectious—consumption is infectious,too, and consump- tion nearly always follows in the train of a neglected cold.

.-..4,-_._. THE Y.M.C.A BAZAAR.

Saturday's Opening Proceedings. The Gtisha Bazaar in aid of the funds ol the Young Men's Christian associatioe, was brought to a close on Saturday evening. The opening ceremony was per- formed in the afternoon by Lady Brook', of Norton Priory. The Mayor (Alderman Roberts) presided, and in addition to several ladies, there were present on the platform :—The Revs J Yonge, J Davies, and T Bishop, and Messrs J Charlton Parr, John Fairclou,eh, J Horton, and S B Stringer. The MAYOR said the duty which bad been allotted t 3 him was not only a very easy one, but a most pleasant one. It was to offer on their behalf, and on behalf of the people of Warrington generally, a most cordial wel- come to Lady Brooke for her presence among them. It was ertremely pleasant to see her ladyship among them at any time, but on an occasion like this, when the object of her visit was to do some permanent good to an institution which had so much claim upon them all, it added to its value and their pleasure in receivinee it. Re did not know that he need say anything in regard to the goad work of the institution in whose support they were met. Young Men's Christian Assoceations in various parts of the couetry had become for many years past a recognized fact, and their needs and claims ban become more and more establi:hed among them every year. He was sorry that the weather and probably the extraordinary claims of the past year had to some extent militated against the success of the bazaar isp to the present, hut there were two mottoes on the top of the page of the day's programme which might tee taken to heart, and if acted upon in a practical manner the re- sult of that day would make up in a great measure for the only partial success of the two previous days. The The first was " Great business must be done this day." It was an instruction which if earnestly and honestly carried out would result well. The other was" Do all that you know and all that you don't"—he did not com- prehend the meaning of the latter part of the sentence; the audience most attach their own construction to it —(laushier)—" no chance must he wasted to-day.' (Applause.) in asking Lady Brooke to open the bazaar he expressed the hope that this would not be the last occasion upon which she would similarly honour the town with a visit. (Applause.) Lady Bacioise said the objects of tbe Young Men's Christian Association must appeal to everyone, and more especially to those who had sons of their own, who in time would have to go out into the world and fight Its difficnities and temptations. Any attempt at work in the direction of training the young must have their sincere support as Englishmen and Englishwomen, for it was upon them that the future of the country rested. (Hear, hear.) She knew that a great re%ny claims had been made upon them during the last _veer, eotn on their purses and sympathy, but she was quite sure that because of having respond, d to them they would not neglect institutions so worthy of their support as this one. (Applause) The crisis through which the country had passed called for renewed efforts on their part, and while recognizing with admiration the splendid services of their men in the field, their magnificent courage, endurance and forbearance under the greatest provocation, they must not forget those lett at home, who, with equal oppertunitv would, no doubt, have done quite as well as those abroad. (Rear, hear.) Se had much pleasure in declaring the bazaar open and wished it the success it deserved. (Applause.) Miss Bessie Dterross in proposing a vote of thanks to Lady Brooke said she was a nearer ceighbour of hers than of many of them, and she ,very ott m saw, heard and knew of her kindly, gracious, helpful work. She came in contact with it personally sometim sat the Runcorn Workhouse, and could testify to her ladyship's ready hireiliness in every good thing in which she could assist. Only the previous dakeshe was engaged in a similar work in the old parish close to her own gates. Mr James HORTON, as one of the originators of the Y.M.C.A., and a member of the present committee seconded the vote of thanks.

Ths, motion was heartily clrried. On the motion of Mr J "C PARR, Seconded by Mr H PIGOT, a vote of thanks was accorded to the Mayor for presiding.

TOTAL PROCEEDS. The total ntreeeds of the bazaa7 amounted approxi- mately to £350.

A Narrow Escape.—Scene at the Central Station.

An exciting scene was witnessed at the Central Station on :lionday evening. The 5.50 express train for Liverpool was leaving the station when a man attempted to get into one of the carriages. He was unsuccessful and fell between the carriage and the platform and was dragged a short distance. One of the railway officials who was close at band saw the accident, and with no little difii,ulty extricated him from his dangerous position.

THE EXAMINEIL

DECEMBER 14TH 1900.

The House of Commons has not expressed its disapproval of either the family.espect of the Government or of the connec- tion of members of the Administration with com- panies directly and indirectly concerned in Government contracts. But this reluctance to censureis not the end of the matter, and the com- ments of respectable journals are worth a little consideration. The .Standard is always independ- ent in it&comments. on public affairs, and it re-

- Family 'Politics.

marked on the two, debates: "To put the matter briefly in both cases, we could have wished that Lord SALISBURY had been able to obtain more talent outside the family circle, and that Mr. CHAMBERLikaN held no shares in any public com- pany to which Government contracts were given." Ehe Morniv _Poe passed over the amendment about the family aspect of the Govern- ment, but it said.: "We agree with the spirit of the (contract) amendment, so far as it aims at re- affirming the probity of British public life," though protdsting against the use of it as "an engine to injure MT. CHAMBERLAIN." The Times, without committing itself to any judgment on the conwosition of the Government beyond declar- ing that Mr. GERALD ,1341/FOUR " was certainly not zo conspicuous a success at the Irish Office thatkle had any specialclaim to be promoted into the t.Cliabinet as President of the Board of Tradn;" suggested that a Prime Minister is not " necaocarily above the influes.ce of personal pre- possession, or even Qf family feeling." A Prime Ministe7, we were also informed, is not" necess-

arily stac.:nger in the Cabinet because there are ,aeveraf =embers of it who are closely connected with himby birth or &Mance." We turn to the

World which cannot be sregarded as biassed in ;favour of any Liberal Iriew of aftairs, and we find .it stated "The feeling of dissatisfaction is very great" about the family appointments. The ,Frinciple f family selem.'-ion is condemned. "It introduces .2sa element of insecurity into public life. The surviving members of the Ministry must feel that their sitcation is precarious, and 'that each holds his office only until Lard.SALEURY wants it for nome'aody The question they must be askißg each othw is Whoseturn will it be next? There,are, we believe, in the House of Commons two members of tile Cecil family as yet officially ;unprovided for." Yhese comments are signifi- cant botause they glew that general attention is 6eing directed againa the composition of the Government, and they justify the remark that, if the Ministry had Lleen reconstructed before instead of after the General Election the electors wcald have bon disposed to be Less

ready to snpport.it. flge Daily Telegraph had no comments on the zontstitution of the Government, end on the contract amendment it declared that it would be monstrous to contend that no Minister should be directly or indirectly concerned "with a.ny commercial money-mating business." The amendment did nob make any such restriction, by the way, as it was limited to contracting firms. But the Telegraph contention was that "a man of honour must be left in this, as in every other instance, to be the keeper of his own conscience." The Daily Mail another journd not friendly to Liberal views, remarked that our "destinies can be, and are, controlled by a single family. If some are disposed to grumble at this let it be understood that this is the way in which the Empire is governed." As to Mr. CHAMBER- LAIN, the admission was made that "he was un- wise in failing to follow himself the rule which he had laid down," and that "a more careful Minister would have avoided even so slight a cause of offence." These quotations will suffice to shew that the questions raised are not of so light and party a character as to be dismissed with con- tempt. Mr. BARTLEY, who attacked the corn- position of the Government, is not a Liberal, but a life-long Conservative, who was once a Con- servative organiser. The contract amendment* represents the quickened view of official life which finds increasing favour with all classes, and in spirit must be accepted as sound, apart from the personal matter with which it was almost neces- sarily connected.
