UNNECESSARY fuss is I The -Commander-in- being made about the Chief. conditions under which the new COMMANDER-IN-CHIEF has accepted office. It was hoped, and it was announced, that he had insisted upon receiving the powers which had been exercised by the Duke of CAMBRIDGE, and that he had procured the abolition of the fbmous Minute of E. 895; by which the COMMANDBR-IN-CEEIEF holds office for five years, and shares much of his power with the Minister for War and the National Defence Committee. It is now discovered that no change has been mayle, and there is unreasonable disappointment in consequence, especially amongst persons who are not at the trouble to understand the position. The reforms carried it in 1895 were recommended by a Commission which investi- gated the whole subject, under the presi- dency of the Duke of DEVONSHIRE, then the Marquis of HARTINGTON, after whose then title the Commission was named, and is still referred to. The Commission advanced Ireasons for the change which are as valid now as they were then. A wore efficient Parliamentary control of the army was desired. But this is now being represented as a reduction of the powers of the COMMANDER-IN-CHIEF, not only in adminis- trative matters, but in pronosing:ehanges in our military system. There may have been some re- strictions in the former case, but in the latter the COMMANDER-IN-CHIEF is in as good a position as he was before, even if he be not in a better posi- tion. The autocracy has vanished; but it was never satisfactory, and it would not :accomplish anything the country desires, ,st the present moment, if it were wholly restored. The discipline land care of the British Army is shaped between two officials—the COMMANDER-IN.CHIEF and the SECRETARY for War. The former is mainly re- sponsible, and the latter can now consult the COM-. MANDER-IN-CHIEF directly, and correct his know- ledge by consultation with the heads, of special offices. He could not do this before, and so it is said that the military head of the army is in a de- graded position. This is a ridiculous account of what takes place at the War Office, because the same thing occurs at the other offices as between the Premier and their respective heads. As to the reorganisation of the army, the situation would not be improved by restoring the position before 2E95. When re- forms were wanted at that time the Duke of CAM- BRIDGE could do nothing requiring legislation without the Minister for War and Parliament. Lord ROBERTS will be in exactly the same position. The free hand desired for him in administration he will have; he, cannot have it in large reforms, and it is undesirable he should have it. The re- presentatives of the country have to sanction what ,he proposes, or otherwise the army would become a real.imperium n imperio, and we might as well hand it back to the Crown and forego the resulta of the two Revolutions which have brought about Parliamentary .as distinct from Rayal control. When Lord 111:)ThERTS has thought out :a new scheme of reform he will have to discuss it with the Government and not with Mr. :BRODRICK alone, and Parliament will have to sanction it. Confidence in Lord ROBERTS should warrant the belief that what he deems best will be sanctioned and accepted. He will have a better and not a worse chance of getting his scheme passed than if he were wielding the powers exercised by the Duke of CAMBRIDGE. Difficulties may occur with the Treasury,but it is for the country to overcome them and not for Lord ROBERTS to override them. Accordingly, the idle chatter about a return to the atatusguo ante 1895 being essential to wig) army reform is simple nonsense.

The new Duchess of Manchester has explained to a newspaper reporter how happy the Duke and herself are in their marriage state, and how indifferent they are to worldly considerations. The two facts which she seems to have been at most pains tdmake dear are that the Duke married, her, a Chicago millionaire's daughter, with a frank indifference to money, and that she herself has something of a •contempt for titles, and could not help it that the object of her affection was hidden under strawberry leaves. It was an odd coincidence certainly, that two people with such a disregard for the advantages of pedigree and wealth should happen to fail in love with each other, and by uniting in marriage give each other the social benefits of both qualifications. Mathematicians would put the chances of such a casual meeting of two pairs of eyes—the one from Chicago and the other from a ducal seat—as somewhat remote, unless they were looking for each other; and that we know was not the case in :this instance. In Vienna and in other parts of Austria, as in North Germany, would-be wooers do not all put a pre- carious trust in coincidences. If a young man, for instance, is clever, and has a good prospect of -making a mark in his profession if he can strengthen his education and start with good social advantages,he informs the feminine world so under cover of a newspaper offer of marriage. The offer must be absolutely honourable; his antecedents must bear investigation—there are far f 3wer marriage frauds abroad proportionately than in England; and then, if the two people seem to have. sympathies in common, a satisfactory marriageis arranged. They act in these cases on the principle that marriage for money is base and revolting, but marriage without it, under some conditions, means a sacrifice of a career—indeed, a wrong done to humanity.

The definite retirement of Mr Edward Lloyd from the concert platform is an event which de- mands more than passing notice. We may have hAd tenors more richly gifted by nature than Mr. Lloyd, or singers who by their brilliant perfor- mances in opera have dyed their reputation in all the hues of romance; but there has been none who has more worthily upheld the classic dignity of English music, or has left behind him a cleaner record of devotion to his art. He might have distinguished himself as the first Eng- lish tenor to sing in " Tanhanser " but a most lucrative engagement was refused through his de- termination not to go upon the stage. In fact, it is said that he has only once sung in costume, at a private house in Hampstead, when he appeared in an operetta of Mendelesolm's. It was in oratorio that he made his mark, and an oratorio soloist ha has remained to this day,holding inithat sphere un- questioned pre-emineuce for nearly thirty years. While on the ballad concert platform he may have rivals ; in the great roles of oratorio he has none. He leaves his professional careei. in excellent voice, and even at 55 years of age might have along period of popularity before him it he chose; but he has determined, and we must think wisely, not to run the risk of lagging superfluous on the stage It is the wise decision of a great artist that there shall be no mean anti-climax in his life-work, and in his retirement to his country estate he will be followed by the good wishes of thousands.

Birds of the air even, according to "Sussex Rector," canlbe satirical at the expanse of our rail- way proprietors, since this is how they have come to regard the gaols on the lines, in his part of the country, as a kind of special providence arranged for their accammJdation. A truck of coal ordered by a gentleman who resides in the neighbourhood of Hastings was so long on the road that when at last it arrived it was found that some of the feathered tribe had built a nest among the coal, in which eggs had been duly laid aun hatched, and the young birds had got away sately.

The Christmas outlook in a certain direction is not too reassuring to the provincial hanie wife. • In addition to the dearth and consequent dear- ness of currants, which will eff set the cost of the Christmas plum pudding, the toothsome mincepie, and all varieties of cakes, it is reported that the dessert table is threatened in a way compared with which calamity, the war would be a trial. Table fruit is plentiful, but there is ona other exception, and that is nucs Tne almonds which from time immemorial have been affianced to the raisins, the popular walnut, the C,181; iron husked Brazil, and the familiar black Spanish have all yielded poorly this season, and will ba found dearer than for many years past. Let us hope that the cheapness of other fruits will make adequate amends.

Warrington is a benighted old town. yet, and the distance it lags behind in the application of modern inventions and appliances to the Fublic convenience is to some extent indicated by an incident which occurred one day this week. The fact that we are dependent on such an antiquated mode of progression as the two-horse 'bus service is in itself evidence of our backwardness, but the incident in this connection here related makes poignant the sufferings we endure. A passenger from Latchford "caught" the omnibils at the terminus and sat down expecting an early and expeditious departure. Ten minutes of silent meditation elapsed and then the diminutive conductor was interrogated. "What time do you start?" queried the pass- enger.—" A quarter past" was the reply— " But the 11.17 train has just gone !—" Oh! we don't go by train time. We have our own time.' (More meditation) and then—" Where is the driver?"—"Avin 'is 'air cut."—Amazed collapse of the questioner. Half-past the hour arrived, and with it the next 'bus, the driver of which calmly transferred himself to the waiting vehicle and drove off, the missing driver being, presumably, still in the hands of the village barber. And we the inhabitants of the County Borough of War-o ringtou, at the dawn of the 20th century endure this! Patience is a virtue. .

It is an ill wind still that blows no good to any- one. Newspapers and other similar merliu:ns are just now being benefited by the pnsoned beer scare, since ithose advertise now who never advertised before, and those who advertised now advertise tht, mole. Mr Ciare Sewell Read, and Sir Cuthbert Quilter, two " doctors" in the sense of Pope's well- known adage, appear determined to make the most of the alarm which has seized the public mind, in order to promote the cause of purer beer. The name of the former has for many years been before the public as that of an eminent actriculturist, and afermer M.P., who supported Sir Cuthbert Q 'liter's fare Beer Bill on a committee appointed to inquire into its merits and the possibility, if approved, of carrying it. It is believed in soo3e quarters that if it-could be brought before Parliament now, it would thave a particularly good chance of getting to the statute book. It was one of Sir Cuthbert's objects to cause it to be eoacte i that beer should be obtainable with a certificate of purity and freedom from injurious ingredients, and, incidentally, that it should be brewed from malt and hops only. In other words the demand was, and is still raised, that beer should be beer and not something else called by that name. It is somewhat of a satire upon the safeguards of our boasted civilisation that chemists and others are under heavily penalised restrictions as to the sale of poisons, whereas beer which is a common drink of the nation, is not sub- ject to any such supervision.

Mr. J. H. Gornall's report on arsenical beer poisoning proves how eliglatly Warrington has been affected by the epidemic which has created suck alarm in Manchester, Salford, Liverpool and other' places. The beer manufactured in Warrington is exonerated from all blame for the few cases of suspected poisoning which do exist, and thie must be counted a feather in the cap of our local brewers. A natural inference therefor° seems to be that the Warrington people so inclined may go on drinking beer to their hearth' content. We certainly do not counsel them to do this, however. Beer at its best contains alcohol, and alcohol is quite poisonous enough to bring about the death of the immoderate consumer. Alcoholic neuritis is little less horrible than that aggravated by arsenic, and it is not an uncommon cause of death in Warrington. Beer-drinkers wouk be better advised if they took warning by recent happuings and cultivated the consumption of a liquid less injurious in itself and less liable to contamination. Milk and water are beverages worth a trial—not mixed of course—even by topers. The latter are quite welcome to our suggestion.

A bad time seems to be coming for what in large towns and cities are known as "orderly boys,"' whatever it may prove to be from the ratepayerst point of view. It comes in the shape of a paten scavenging machine, calculated to render the orderly boys a superfluity. The apparatus, which is an American invention, can be pushed by a man, and it will do the work of four or five boys. It will soon be at work on the wood-paved streets in the centre of Birmingham. It is not known whether the local High way Department has any portent of very arctic .weather this winter there,but it appears that not only has i arranged for a number of extra snow "tips," but it has been ordering more snow ploughs. Birmingham has often proved itself a place which,in municipal matters,other towns might safely copy, but these are certainly "orders" which would be " large " for most communities.

"Forced, foolish, futile." were the three graphic words in which Mrs. Allan-Bright summarized the results of the resent .general election in her address to the members of the Warrington Women's Liberal Association last week. She had little difficulty in showing that these words, with their trio of " ", were an accurate summary of the Government's achievement at the rccent Parliamentary election. The address was an admirable one and was listened to with interest and profit by the large gathering of ladies present. It was a matter for regret that the sterner sex was not batter represented but perhaps this was due to an impression that they were not " wanted " on this occasion. Not only was the audience favoured with an address from Alrs.:Bright but her husband, who was the Liberal candidate for Exeter at the recent election, also spoke. In the speaking line, Mrs Bright was certainly the "better half" on this occasion. Nevertheless Mr. Bright made an earnest protest against the manner in which the war in South Africa is being conducted.

MATTERS OF MOMENT.

A Provincial Letter.

Juries are supposed, by an impossible and curious fiction of our legal procedure, to dismiss all sym- pathy and prejuaice from their minds while in the "box "or the jury room. Jurymen, however, are but men after all, and the evidence to which they sometimes listen must of itself create sympathy, if. not prejudice. Anyhow, an accident reported from the Edmonton County Court this week is typical not only of the relations which sometimes exist be- tween jurymen and judges, but of the way in wbich the former occasionally assert their sym- pathy. The case itself was simple, and the jury desired at its conclusion to give effect to their sympathy with one of the suitors. CariouslT enough the sympathy of the judge was on the same side, but the man of law gave his decision as such,while the jury sought to give a verdict in accordance with their feeling of sympathy. But the .judge re- fused to accept the verdict, and entered judgment according to his view of the law. Thereupon a juror, probably with the full concurrence of the other occupants of the box,exclaimed that he could see no use in their coming there at all, if they had only to decide as the judge directed. The judge made good tempered allowance for this little ebulli- tion, which, after all, gave expression to a very popular misunderstanding, and one that affords some thoughts worth pondering over.

Many and important are the preparations now in progress for the census of Great Britain. which is to be taken on Sunday, March 31st. The enumera- tion of the population is taken on the Sabbath be- cause people are supposed to be at home on that day. Within the decade the poiulation has in- creased enormously, and no fewer than forty thou- sand enumerators will be engaged in the work next year, or three thousand more than in 1891. It is evidently not everyone who is qualified to fill the office of enumerators. A.ccording to the fairly"high' standard fixed by Somerset House, he must be "in-' telligent, trustworthy, active,write well, must. have some knowledge of arithmetic, must not be infirm, or of weak health, not under eighteen or over sixty- five, must be temperate, orderly and respectableond be suil a person as is likely to conduct himsPlf with strict propriety and civility in the dischary of his duties." All these qualifications, together with a week's work,may bring the happy poisessor the magnificent sum of about thirty-five shillings.

For the "first time in its history, probably,the War Office is actually proming for the future. From the announcement which has been made this week, it appears that the gratuities ofbred to the return- ing troops, Imperial awl Colonial, who have been employed in South Africa, will range from five pounds upwards. Reservists and volunteer o will obtain ten to fifteen pounds, awl captains sixty pounds. The War Office has, moreover, taken a step that will greatly advance the effectiveness of our forces in the field. An order has been issued that after the commencement of the New Year every officer of Cavalry or Infantry shall, before he is pro- moted to the rank of lieutenant, be required to obtain the officer's extra musketry certificate. It is presumed that this order is applieable both to regu- lars and Volunteers,and to the latter it should prove decidedly encouraging. It is hoped in the provinces that the War Office will reauire not only a theoreti- cal knowledge of musketry from the officers, but practical demonstration that they have profited by what they have acquired on paper. It will be better for the men.

It has more than once been observed that in matters of municipal administration Glasgow can give lessons to many parts of the Engli-h provinces. Here is one of its recent arrangements for getting rid of the waste paper which too often litters the streets everywhere. The Corporation has just established a regular separate collection from shop- keepers and others, providing them with bags, and calling for them *ten full with a covered van. Off era were invited for what was thus gathered up,

It seems probable that superstition may accom- plish what humanity has failed to effect; for it is reported that Paris ladies are giving up the use .of " o,preys "or aigrettes, in millinery and for hair- dressing, because they are supposed to hasten the growth of crows' feet around the eyes of their wearer'. Bird-lovers ought to take steps to inoculate English women, of every degree with the same kind of notion.

This Club held its weekly lecture, on Tuesday night under the presidency of Mr Thomas Lei. The lecturer, was W Millington, and in a brilliant manner he dealt with the " Reform Bill of 1867." Toe lecture is perhaps best de.icribed as a fascinat- ing page o history, the conclusion of which would leave no doubt as to Who passed the Bill. Teat was a purely Liberal measure is clearly ineicated Lord Salisbury's testimonial to Mr Gladstone, for the then Lord Cranbourne stated "Mr Gladstone had practically dictated the particulars of the Measure to the Government." The lecturer urged that the work of the Liberal Party as reformers was not complete, and illustrated that, a great de3l more was to be accomplished in the direction of ameliorating the con ition of the working man. In the after discussion Messrs E liniveton, Massey, J Lea, T Wood, J Brown, the Chairman, and °titers took an active part. Tee usual votes of thanks followed. PRESHNTITION To A. RUNCORN BANDSIIIiN. The Drill Hall. Raueora, was the scene of an interesting function on Saturday night last, when Mr. George Booth, Balfour-street, a bandsman of twenty years standing, was the recipient of a valu- able cruet, as a Luken of the regard and esteem of his fellow b indsmen. Prior to the presentation there was a knife and fork tea to which a large com- pany sat down, amongst the number being the popular officer, Captain George F Ashton, Captain Timmins, and Sergeant-Instructor Callaghan, and Sergeant Mort. Bandmaster T Outnwaite, in making the presentation, referred in eulogistic terms to the services rendered by Mr Booth on the solo trombone, and wished him every success in his new sphere of labour at Port Sunlight.—Mr. Booth suitably res- ponded.—Mr. James Moorfield than made a capit-al speech showing the progress of the band. —The remainder of the evening was spent in a convivial manner, songs being contributed by Messrs R Shaw' W Outhwaite, C Garrett; a pianoforte solo by %V Stanway, and a cornet solo by W Gregory.—The toasts included "The Loyal," "The Bandmaster," and "Our comrades at the front," the latter being musically honoured.--Votes of thanks were accorded Mr and Mrs Chamberlain, the host and hostess, fur their admirable catering. The success of the even- ing was in a great measure due to Mr R Shaw, the baud secretary.

The Winding up of a Local Company. At the Warrington County Court on Thursday before his Honour Judge Bowen Rowlands, a petition was heard under the Companies Act 1862' to 1893 against W Furness and Company, Limited, on behalf of the North Shore Alin Cmpany, who were creditors to the extent of £72 23 61, for a winding up order under the provisions at the Companies Act or that such order he made in the promises as shall be just. Mr Lorraine, barrister, Liverpool (in- structed by Messrs Evans, Lockett and Company) appeared for the petitioners, and Mr. Eastwood, barrister, Manchester (instructed by Mr Arthur Browne), represented the company. In opening the case on behalf of the petiti )nors Mr. Lorraine stated that., the firm of William Furness and Com- pany was incorporated in 1892, with registered oilleies in Haydock-street, Warrington, The nominal capital of the company was £5,000 divided into 1,000 shares of £5 each, but the number of shares i=sued was only 240 at.. £5 each. The peti- tion was for a compulsory windiog up of the Com- pany. On the 4th of July last the company passed a special resolution to wioil up the company volun- tarily, and Mr 11 Bowdeo Furness was appointed as liquidator. This gentleman was a near relation of Mr William Furness, who was the sole director of the company. At the time it was agreed to voluntarily wind up the com- pany; it was also decided to re-construct the concern, and the effect of that would be likely to po3judice the interests of Cie unsecured creditore. Mr William Farness was the sole director of the company and under this voluntary liquidation he had appointed his brother as liquidator. He sub- mitted that under the circumstances, the rights of the unsecured creditors were likely to 1:).3 prejudiced and this was essentially a Casts in which theie ought. to be a compulsory winding-up.—Mr.Eastwood said the company had no objectian to the affairs being properly investigated and were quite willing to consent to an order for the co:npuisory winding up of the company•—liis Honour said he would make a compulsory winding-op order.--31r.Uns- ; worth said he appeared in support of the petition. I on behalf of M,ssrF.. Fairelough and Sons and asked ;for his costs to be allowed.—This was granted.

Intuition is the feminine of guess. The well-bred man is never rude uninten- tionally. Nothing is werse for a society reputation than to be suspected of wishing to please. A RESPONSIBLE POSITION.—A mistress told her maid, Bettie, that she must not do things on her own responsibility, but first ask permission. The next day Bettie walked into the parlour and said, politely: "Please, madam, the cat is eating the duck in the pantry; must I stop her?"

SACERDOTALISM. The Present Condition of the Christian Church.

Sermon by the Rev E, T. Reed

Pre,achin:.; on I.)ceember 2ad in the Eminanurl Reformed Church, Warrington, the Rev. E. I. Reed spoke in terms specially applicable to the occasion, which was the 27-h anniversl:ry of the organiz ition of the Reformed Episcopal Church His text was "Is there not a cause," Ist Samuel, 17, 29, and after tracing the history of the Reformed Church, and that of the Emmanuel Church in Warrington, Mr. fired alluded to the spi%.:ad of Sacerdotalisin and priest-craft in the Christian Church to-day. We extract too followi n from his remark; : And what is the condition nf the Christian Chareh in England at the present time ? Christians are divided into three great classes, Viz: First, those identified with the Established Church in this country. Sec-mdly, those who belong to other Protes:ant and Reform6d Christian Churches and who a:te usually called " Nonconformists," and thirdly, Roman Catholics.

In the Established Church there are three parties. First, the High Church or Ritualistic or Sacerdotal party largely the result of the Oxford movement, whose weekly organ is "The Church Times." The English Church Union exists for the purpose of propagating the views of this party. becondly, the Evangelical party whose object it is to uphold the doctrines of grace, the Protes- tant and Reformed character of the Church of England. It is represened in the press by such papers as " The English Churchman,"" Toe Rock." etc. The Church Association has for its object the maintenance and diss- emination of the principles and views of this party. Thirdly, the Broad Church party which is numerically the smallest of the three, and whose theological position it is somewhat difficult to define. Between the High Church or Ritualistic or Sacerdotal party, and the Evangelical or Protestant or Reformed party there is constant conflict. Upon certain points of doctrine they are diametrically eppesed. The two syetems, viz., the Sacerdotal on the one band, and the Evangelic at on the other are quite irreconcileable. The Church of England at the present time is a house divided against itself, and tloe fate of such a house awaits it. Either one party or the ite her must ultimately oust its epponent, and so far as appearances go, it would seem as though the Sacerdotal party were now much the stronger of the two. Notwithstanding tie most strenuous opposition on the part of Evangelicals, by means of the Church Association inside the law courts, and throughout the country, the Sacerdotal party has steadily hut surely pressed forward until the English Church Union has connected with it at the present time more than 4,000 clergy—bishops,- deans, archdeacons and priest', and lay members and associates numbering 35,000. At the head of this solid and formidable body is Lerd Halifax, an able and experienced leader. The manifesto rrcently put forth by this Union through its President, c:early indicates the position taken up upon one important point, viz., the presence of Christ in the Holy Communion. It is as fellows :—" The bread and wine through the operation of the Holy Ghost, become in and by consecration, verily and indeed the body and blood of Christ, and that Christ our Lord, present in the same most holy sacrament of the altar under the form of bread and wine is to be worshipped and adored."

"This," says Archdeacon Taylor of Liverpool, " is absolutely the dcctrine of the Council of Trent as re- gards the bodily presence of Christ in the sacrament, but is virtually transubsrantiation also, albiet the word is not used. In accordanco with this doctrine they de- mand, and, as far as they are permitted, practise, the six points of the Ritualistic charter-vestments, altar light, eastward p,isition, inc:hs,, mixed chalice, and wafer bread. Masses and nrayers for the dead are openly in full swing, and the sacrament of penance or auricular confession and sacramental absolution." "[here is not the least necessity," Archdeacon Taylor goes no to say, "for round table conferences or abortive cor.trivancea of that eort to try and reconcile Evangeli- cal Christianity and Romanism, a deep and broad leav- age the two. The bridge is not made which vidl unite th( m• In one word, the doctrines of the Ritualists are simply Romanism under an alias."

Until recently Nor conformists were not greatly moved by this internecine warfare between the S tcerdotal and Evangelical parties in the Es- tablished Church. Feeling secure in their own freedom from the errors of priestcraft they too much ignored the growing evil. The publication of Mr. Walsh'z book entit.ei " The Secret History of the Oxford Movement" undoubtedly did much to rouse the Protestant part of the nation outside the Eitablished Church to a real appreciation of the peril to the nation caused by that movement. Since then much has been said and done to spread information and to give instruc- tion. Excellent service has been rendered by the Rev. Dr. Horton by means of his little book entitlsd " Eng- lard's Dingcr " by Dr. Fairttairn and others, and many Christians who were formerly indifferent are now ready to resist Sac?rdotalisrn and to stand by the principles of Protestantism and the Reformation. For his faithful testitneny in the House of Commons, to these great and important principles, the tianks of all Evangelical Christians are duo to Mr. Samuel Smith, 51. P , and their prayers should continually be e,ffared that he may re- ceive Divine strength and guidance in the future. The Federation of the Evangelical Free Churches of this country has provided an organisation through which effective opposition may be offered to error and active assistance rendered to aggressive evangelical sniritual eff ‘rt by which alone these errors can be displaced,

What posirion than dos the Reformed Episcop3l Church occupy with respect to these various sections of Chrstians

To the doctrines and principles of the Sacerdotal party it can offer only the most uncempromising opposition. It believes that instead of receiving the Bread of Life by means of a priestly system and ornate services, it is receiving a stelae, and the Reformed Episcopal Church effers to all stich misguided and unsatisfied souls the pure Word of God,a scriptual liturgy freed from It omish error, and a simple worship which does not hinder the soul from having direct access to God. With respoct 4o the Evangelicel party, the Reformed Episcopal Church acknowledges the good they are doing in difficult circumstances. That there are many clergy and laity within the Established Church who have no sympathy with sacerdotalism and are in full accord with the principles and doctrines of Protestautism and the Reformation is an undoubted fact, but we feel that they are sadly hampered and hindered in their efforts by the fact that they remain in a church in which clergy and laity holding views diametrically opposed to theirs are also allowed te propagate their pernicious doctrines and practices. We fee!, moreover, that while the Book of Common Prayer is, upon the whole Evangelical and Prot, s'ant yet it also contains remarks of sacerdotal error which, so long as they remain there, will produce like results. We are of the opinion that instead of seendine our lives in a conflict which up to now has re- sulted in defeats and humiliations for the Evangelical party, wo shall do more effective work by building up a living spiritual church upon the sure foundation of the Word of God, a liturgy treed from Romish eerier, and a censtitution'which allows to the laity as well as to the clergy a full share in the goventneet of the church. Our attitude towards the Sacerdotal party in the Church of England defines our attitude towards the errors of the Church of Rome. We an of opinion that Roman Catholics allow themselves to hold many errors, along with the substratum of truth upon which their seetem i 3 built' What our attitude is towards all other Christian people cannot he better declared than in the words of Bishop Cammins on the occasion of the organization of our church. He then said :—" Towards all other Christie,' people, of like precious faith, our attitude is that only of love, of sympathy, and of earnest desire to co-operate with them in the extension of the kingdom of the Redeemer—both theirs and ours. We regard our movement only as a step towards the closer union of all Evangelical Christendom. For this we shall labour and pray. We gladly acknowledge the validity of the ministerial orders of our brethren whom God has sent into His vineyard, ard whose labours He has accepted and blessed. We shall invite ministers of all Evange- lical churches to occupy our pulpits and to take part in our services. And we shall rejoice to meet them and rhi in flecks as often as may be expedient around the Lord's ;table, ar d acknowledge that" we, being many, are one body in Christ, and members, one of another." Our text asks the question : Is there not a cause? It was David the shepherd who asked it. The armies of God were confronted by the armies of the Philistines with Goliath the giant of their champion .The Philistines were powerful and defiant. The Isnaelities were cowed and humiliated. Bit the man for the time and the occasion had been raised up and God delivered His people by means of David and his sing and stones. We, to-ea,', may ask the same question : Is there not a cause ? I have attempted to answer it. And it may be that in our own day and generation God will use our Reformed Episcopal Church to cast down the hoary. he ided giant of Sacerdotalism which has once again in our beloved land set at defiance the armies of the living God.

Wc are no strong or powerful numeri^ally,financially, or socially. We can be true, we can put ourselv( s ahgolutPly at God's disposal. and iu depondenco upon Him we cart ",ca forward and do a grand work,"

NORTHERN UNION.

Warrington V. Aitrincham.

Warrington played their return match with Altrincham on Saturday, and with the exception of Fish were fully represented. The attendence at the Devisdale enclosure was satisfactory, considering the counter attraction at Stockport, where several of the Altrincham players were engaged. Isherwood, losing the toss, was deputed to commerce against the wind, and the opening phases of the game were witnessed in the Altrincham quarters. Twice the visiting bac'-er broke away, but accurate tack- ling upset calculations. Barnett cleared out the invaders, and a beautiful movement between Duckworth, Isherwocd, Dickenson, and Hockenhall saw the last- named open the seoring,placing the ball behind the posts for Isherwood to place a goal. The locals improved some- what after this reverse, but their e!Zorts lacked method, 3nd Hallam had little difficulty in keeping them in check. ===== 0002601_19001214_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
, Emmanuel Church.—Missionary Services and Meeting.

The Rev. ALBERT EVANS, of the China Inia.ed Mission, who is now on turlough in England aecompanied by his wife, preached at Emmanuel Church, Bold-street, on Sunday, There were good conpregatinne, and the rev. gentleman's discourses were listened to with great attention.—On Monday evening a well attended public meeting was eetel in the schoolroom over A rpley Station. Mr and Mrs Evans appeared in Chinese costume and de- liver'd interesting addressee on the work of the mission. A nunaher of Chinese curios were exhibited. In the course of his opening remarks the Rev. E. T. Reed (vicar) who presided, said they regarded it as a great honour that Mr. and Mrs. Evans were their guests on this occasion, and for this reason, that the canine of the missionary was the highest, the most self-sacrificing the most beneficent of all callings. They believed with Dr. Samuel Smiles that " The career of a missionary is the most dutiful and heroic of all He carries his life in his hand. He braves danger and death. He lives amongst savages, sometimes amongst cannibals. Money could not buy- the devotion with which he encounters peril and miery. Be is only untr-11 by the mission of mercy with which he is charged." (Applause.) The missionary was engaged in co-operating with God in carrying out His great purpose of bringing blessing to the whole world through the Gospel of our Lord and Saviour Jecu-r Christ. The station at which the laboure of Mr. and Mrs. Evans had recently been carried on was no Les' then two ref 1.• sand miles from Shanghai, in the Province of Western China. Altogether, in coming home they travelled over a distance of thirteen thousand miles by land and river and sea. (Applause). Mrs. Evatas then gave an address with reminiscences of her life among the Chinese. During the cours, of her remarks she expressed the hope that they worild have the joy of welceming some of her hearers in the mission field (Applause),—Mr. Evans, at the outset, referred to the present cendition of China l and the cause of all the terrible cart:reeks and , awful messacrea which had taken place. On landing in England he learnt to his surprise that the Press had been ' very liberally blaming the poor missionary for all the troubles in China, and everywhere he found h s fellow. workers on furlough trying to defend the miasiena y and missionary work from the charges brought against them. If they regarded the outrages as caused by the missionary, and thought he was to blame, they were very far wrong. It seemed to him that it required only a very superficial study of the subject to see that the outbreaks were very much more anti-foreign anti politi- cal than they were anti-missionary or religious nerßeeh- tient+. The majority of them left a part of China not because the country was in a state of unrest, or great massacres had taken place, but because a great political movement was in progress. A great anti-foreign rising I was spreading all over the country, and they felt it vr as I not merely a question of their own personal sa.fety, but a question of saving the converts from persecution by l their abser cc. The troubles in China were against the missionary not because he was a missionary hut because he was a foreigner. That opinion was widely shared by those who knew anything about the present condition of China. If he were asked why they went to carry on missionary w Irk in China he sle.ull briefly warmer the question by referring to the words of the 16:h chapter of St Mark "Go and preach the gospel unto all nations." Pro- ceeding the speaker referred in a highly interesting way to the characteristics of the Chinese, including their perseverance, thriftiness and superstition The pro- ceeeings concluded with the singing of the Doxology.

WARRINGTON AND THE BURIAL ACT, 1900.

[By COUNCILLOR F. R. ROBERTS, M.A., LL.B.]

The new Burial Act comes into force on the Ist, of January, 1901. Until then the status quo is main- tained. As regards churchyards the act does not apply at all. It is therefore necessary simply to consider what effect the act will have in moditying the law now governing the rights and remuneration of ministers of religion in respect, of interments in cemeteries, the word " minister" being employed to iticlade all types and classes of persons entitled by law to officiate at such interments. it will be convenient to state generally that the main objects of the Act are (1) to abolish the fanciful and absurd methods of paying ministers of religion for interments, now in force : and (2) t 3 secure that the person actually officiat- ing shall alone receive the pay. hitherto the extent of the remuneration has been partially disguised under the terms " surplice fee "—a fee amount- ing in some cases to as much as us 21 ; the us being presumably for the clergyman and the 2d for the washerwoman—a fee paid to the clergyman in ad lition to the fee which nakedly appeared as the clergyman's. These fees have evidently been reearded as payable tolthe clergyman in right of his office and not in respect of his services ; hence it matters not whether the clergyman, his eurute, or a Non conformist minister does the work ; in all cases the incumbent is paid the fees. Now the Burial Act 1900 rudely disturbs this happy state ot things and in the most vulgar and democratic way possible en- dorses the apostolic dictum that if a man will not work neither shall he eat, It will be convenient to consider the Act as it affects(l) consecrated ground, and (2) unconsecrated ground. (1) It will have been gathered from what has been said, that under certain circumstances a Non- conformist minister could and can inter in con- secrated ground. Until 1880,however it was not so. But by the Burial Act 1880, a Nonconformist minis- ter might be employed by his people to inter in consecrated ground. Of course the question may be asked why a Di,senter should wish to be so interred To this the answer is that in articalo a man may and sometimes does attach importance to the "odour of sanctity," and accordingly may express a desire for consecrated burial. But in the great majority of such interments the simple explanation is that some member of the deceased's family father, mother, wife, or child— may lie buried in a consecrated grave and the wish to be put by their side is but one oE the dictates of natural affction. Until 1880 the Dissenter pat into such a grave had to be buried by consecrated hands and his own minister had either to stay away or stand by as a spectator. But though the Act accorded this measure of justice to Nonconformists it also provided that the burial fees should as before go to the clergyman. This was one of those compi omises so dear to the British statesman's heart, compromises which, whilst admitting a wrong, compensate the man who does it. And now we get at the gist of the new Act. In new cemeteries , all ministers of religion are to receive the fees which the Act compels the authorities—in our case the Town Council—to fix by way of remuneration for actull services rendered. That is to say that the Rev. Smith, Brown, Jones,or Robin- aon,the Episcopalian,the Nonconformist, the Roman Catholic, all alike will receive one fee ; a fee which will not be masqueraded under the name of " sur- plice," " clerk " or any other disguise but will be fixed, plain, and unmistakable. So far for new cemeteries, whether the ground ha consecrated or unconsecrated. _ - -

But with regard to old cemeteries (i e. in being before 1901) whilst the new fees will apply to all ministers of religion burying in unconsecrated ground the rights (legally so called) of the present incumbents of the town are safeguarded in the following manner ;—For 15 years from the Ist January next such incumbents will be entitled not only to the old fees but to all fees now payable in respect of interments in constcrated ground by whomsoever the interments are carried out. If such incumbents lie beyond the 15 years then their rights are preserved so long as they live. (Some doubt exists as to whether in a Cuss of such in- cumbents dying or resigning before the expiration of 15 years their successors, will take their places under the Act for the unexpired portion of the 15 years. To my mind, however, it is clear that such successors would be so privilegeJ.) (2) Little need be said about unconsecrated gronad. All ministers of religion whether incum- bents or dissenters will be entitled only to the fee now fixed by the Cemetery Committee, and only then if they have in person discharged the duties at the grave aide. A great deal has been said about the scale of fees, and I understand that one or two of our local clergy are somewhat diF-- mayed by the prospect before them. Why? Is it a question of vested interests? Then the 15 years' enjoyment of the present rate of pay whether earned or not should satisfy any reasonable man. A lironconfornoist might even be tempted to go further and say that com- pulsory fees whether for services rendered or not are repugnant to the religious spirit. But we will let that pass. Assuming that tee 15 years' exemp- tion had no existence and that the new scale ap- plied at once could it be said that ministers of religion had been harshly treated ? Let us consider the question just one moment. It appears that in the pass one scale of fees has been payable in res- pect of a morning and another for an afternoon in- terment. For some occult reason known only to the clergy, a much higher fee has been charged in respect of the morning than of the afternoon in- terment, such moraines fee (with the aid of the sur- plice and the wastierwoman),in some cases amount- ing to 325; whilst in the afternoon the fee has been on the average 3; 61. Curi- ously enough fees have varied in proportion to the distance of the grave from the cemetery chapel, contemptible as such a method of assessment annears to be. What has been the result of this differentiation ? That the interments in the morn- ing have been very few—some 30 per annum —and confined exclusively to the well-to-do. The boast of thelChurch of England that she is the church of the poor is not borne out in this instance. By her heavy demands at the graveside she has given

the rich a monopoly of the quiet morning hours. in ninety-nine cases out of one hundred therefore the tees paid to the clergyman for burials have been payable lin respect of afternoon services, about 3s 6d. per interment.

What does the npw scheme propose? For morn- ing interments a fee of lOs ; for afternoon 3s.

did my best both in the co.nmittee and in the Council to reduce the lOs to is 6d. For this I have been severely taken to task. I am asked if I should care to meet a client in the neighbourhood of the Cemetery in a morning for 7s 6d. My reply is that at all events I should get the same fee whether morning or afternoon. But that is not the question. It one had to argue the point I should say that the whole system of burial fees ought to be swept away, and that ministers of religion should discharge these sad offices for such reward as the affection and respect of the living would bring them. But so long as the law requires that a fee shall be paid, the only question for us is what is a fair fee? I contend that the suggested scale is fair having in mini not only the incomes of the clergy but also the grinding poverty of the poor. Let it be remembered that from inquiries made by the committee the scale proposed here is seen to be in advance of any scale yet adopted under the Act by any town. For testi- mony I refer your readers to the statement by the chairman of the committee at the Council meeting. In conclusion may I say that in a report which has appeared of my remarks at the Warrington Town Council meeting I am made to say things which certainly I never did say. Your own report, which I have seen, is accurate, and I abide by it, and I think your readers will agree that it is consistent with what I have written here. F. R. R JBERTS.

DEATH AND FUNERAL OF MRS. KIERNAN.

The news ot. the death of Mrs Kiernan, proprietress of the Patten Arms Hotel, Warrington, will come as a painful surprise to many in Warrington and district. On Tuesday last week she was found dead in bed at he residecce, Borholm, Tnelwall. Abut a month ago the deceased lady was taken ill, and was attended by Dr. White.but she recovered and seemed to have many years of useful life before her. There was no indication that the end was so near and her sudden death came as a shock to her family and relatives. Her position as proprietress of the Patten Arms Hotel introduced her to a wide circle of frien is and acquaintances by whom she was greatly rsepected and esteemed. She was the wife of the late Mr Thomas Bernard Kiernan, vs ho pre- deceased her aoout two anl-a-half years, and was the daughter of the late Mr B Ficnigan, of Manchester. Sne was 57 years of age. The grave closed over the remains of the deceased lady on Friday, at the Warrington Cemetery. Fatties Baynes. The other clergy present were the Rev. Dean Finnegan, of Apple- ton, Rev. letters the Obttor.

Lights on Vehicles.

Sir,— I notice in your report of the Town Council pro- ceeding of last weok that "owing to th. Watch Corn- =Me having received a memorial from thc carriers and Carting agents asking them to so alter the bye-law with respect to lights on vehicles, that it shall only apply to vehicles proceeding faster than walking paw.," that the Town Clerk has been instructed to obtain informatim as to what is done in ether towns. On behalf of the vast number of cyclist in the War- rington district allow me to most emphatically protest against such a procneding. The National Cyclists Union and the Cyclista 'Touring Club have spent valuable time and large sums of money in tryirg to get thol Universal Lights on Vehicles Bill passed through Parliament, but Owing to pressure of other business and the small amount of time allowed for private measures, unfortun- ately they have not,m.t with the success they so greatly deserve. The County Councils and also a large number of the city and Town Councils (of which Warrington is one) have helped to mak. matters easier by passing bye- laws in their own respective districts which practically 'fleet the case. Now, at the insdzation of a few cart owners who want to saver a paltry shilling or two in oil per annum, our Watch Committee is asked to alter a bve-law which ' has proved invaluable,and thug jeopardize the lives and limbs of hundreds of Warrington ratepayers who happen to be the unfortunate (?) possessors of cycles, and who have ta habitually use the streets after dusk. Unlighted vehicles are just as dangerous to pedestrians as cyclists, especially in the magnificently (?) illumin- ated, and superbly (?) surfaced atreets of the town and Surely some measure of protection is due to them as well as wheelmen. A slowly moving lampless vehicle is simply a lumbering Juggernaut to wheelmen and pedestrians alike, and I hope that when the matter is brought up again before the Council, that they as a boay,will not listen to any alteration of the existing bye- law whatever, as I am firmly convinced that any sane person who objects to vehicles having a light attached after dusk is totally unfit to act as a local legislator. Thanking you for your valuable space, and with best Wishes, I remain, yours truly, E. PICKAVANCE, jun., Hon. Sec. Warrington Cycling Club, Vice-Chairman National Cyclists Union (Liverpool Centre). December 11th, 1900.

Christmas Cheer for Ironworkers.

Sir,—l have been consulted by a large number of friends (who have already promises of several sub- scriptions) whether some sort of Christmas cher:. could Inot be given to the many ironworkers who have almost been out of employment for the last three or four months and so far as I can see will not be in any better position for some months to come. No doubt you will think ironworkers' wages are very high. That I admit, but what is the benefit of high wages if there is no work 13 be done. I personally know of families at the present time who are in great straits for want of fire and food and many cases have come under my observation of people wh) have had to seek relief from the Board a! Guardians so that a little help at the present time would be highly appreciated. Donations in money, fond, or clothing will be thank- fully received by the committee, and acknowledged. ....Thanking you in anticipation. THOMAS B. mALEIN. 2, Lilford-street, Warrington.'

Arsenical Beer Poisoning. The Medical Officer's Report on Warrington.

Sir,—Poisoning by arsenical beer, as will already be generally known, has not occurred in the borough of Warrington to any great extent. Neuritis, of which ths direct couse is excessive con- sumption of alcoholic liquors is of caurse in a town like Warrington not infrequent, but the fact that during the quarter ending 30'h September only one death was regis- tered as due to alcoholic neuritis is sufficimt indication that during the period when the prevalence of arsenic poisoning in neighbouring towns was greatest, there was no abnormal increase of this class of cases in Warrington. Moreover, enquiries elicit the fact that though some medical men report an increase in the number of cases of neuritis under treat- ment, the greater number of these are outside the borough, and are not due to liquor of Warrington manufacture. The cases in public institutions tthe Workhouse and Infirmary) tboug.h some I believe pre- Senting signs that indicate an arsenical origin, are not numerous. Numerous samples of beer have been examined since the matter came to the front, and a few only have been found to contain slight pace; of arsenic : indeed the quantity contained is so minute as to render it im- possible to estimate it.

Municipal Offices, Warrington.

Corporation Contracts and Accounts. Sir,—ln you, contemporary of last Saturday a letter was published from Mr. Martin Flanagan in which he takes me to task for suggesting that we bad tome in- dependent members of the Town Council. I can only now say that when I wrote that letter I truly and firmly believed that we had several ; I can assure him and the public that I bad the very best and proper reasons to believe so but alas! I must admit I made a mistake. At the meeting last Tuesday of the Town Council, when I and many other ratepayers expected so much from Several of our representatives, that business ended in quite s farce for the reason that Council'or For- shaw's motion adds nothing new to the situation, far what he proposed has been the law many years. It does, however, show this, that M: Fershaw must have had some imforrnation which caused him to move in the matter. He, being a lawyer, would know that without his moving such a resolution, the law had provided for such transactions. If that was so I think it was his duty to extend his motion so that an inquiry could be held to satisfy the public mind. Mr Flanagan Is wrong in his facts in saying that the Ceuncii is en- tirely drawn from the leading manufacturers. This is not eo,for with a few exceptione the councillors are very small ratepayers, in fact I do not supeose there are many towns the same size as ours where the rateable value of it town council is se low. Therefore I must beg to differ with Mr. Flanagan on that point. Mr. Flanagan wishes to drawn my atteetion to the working class ratepayers, whom he remarks I have con- temptuously ignored in my letter, but this is net so and if he will carefully Consider nay let,er he will find that what I did say was "the largest manufacturers tradesmen and ratepayers." Ratepayer, of course, includes tbe working classes. I must agree with him in saying that there are undoubtedly many working men capable of going into the matter and that they do pay a very large share of the rates, and what is more as they have the largest number of votes they are respon- sible for those who now represent the ratrpavere on the Town Council. The audit alai investigation I and many others consider necessary, would take quite eight or ten weeks, and if Mr Flanagan and his friends will get to work and do all they can in the matter no one will be better pleased than the writer. The Mayor has done nothing towards bringing about the inquiry many ratepayers wish for. He has only by his explanation made one of the cape fit, and there are many other caps waiting wearers who I think will be in no hurry to try them on. As there was no resolution at last Tuesday's Council meeting re Auditor's report, I suppose it is the intention of the Council not to publish the Fame. although it his always been done in the past. I want the public to know the reason. It is, in nay opinion, became Auditor's report pointed out two or three undesirahle matters in the accounts, one of which wee the exnenses of deputa- tions, which amounted to £3lO 94 9d, among which were the following : MALTS CONGRESS, SOIITIILMPTON d.

Health Department, Longford Manure Department, Nighteoil Collection De- partment HEALTH CONGRESS, BLACKPOOL . Health Department, Longford Manure Works nepartment 4l 10 0 Hiehway Department to Scotland, Ireland, and various towns Ido not wonder that this report of the Auditors has not been published but as I have said on previous occa 'ions it is time the public had an investigation and they will I am afraid have to look elsewhere than the Coun- cil Chamber to Wet it. Manchestee, Salford, Bolton, Blackpool and other Isues have all been very fortunate in having a Clur- 6;lnr bold enou,h to bring the matter forward, but we in Warrington will, I am afraid, not get such an inquiry excent through the Loral Government Beard. I see that in the Curt of Queen's Bench that Mr Justice Bir ham removed a Councillor for trading to the extent oronly £24 with his Council, although the work was done by his two sons, who, he Said, were in business for themselves. and His Lordship made the followidg remarks:—"He wished to say that his ex- perience showed that there were noon Local Boards far too many of this class of men,builelers and nersons who were likely to be interested in work rf cplired to be done for the local 'lndy. They were remnted to enter into Contracts vrith which they oncht to have nothing to do. He therefore made a declaration that the defenlant was run longer entitled to sit as a member of the Couneil " The transaction in question only amounted to £24 but I think every right minded person will agree with Mr Justice Bighorn,

Your; trilr, JOHN PEMBERTON.

LATEST NEWS.

Christiarity in China. A Convert Tortured. HONG KONG, Thursday. Reports received here from Canton state that Dr. Young has been horribly tortured. He was strung up by thumbs and toes, but confessed nothing. Note.—Dr. Young is apparently one of the Chinese converts for whose capture a reward was offered in an anti-foreign placard which was posted up at Fatshan last month.

A Trap that Failed.

MAFEKING, MONDAY.

It is unofficially states that on Saturday some of the 10th Yeomanry and sth Fusiliers under Colonel Meyrick were heavily engaged at Wonderfontein, A messenger was sent to Lord Methuen at Kaffirs' Kraal, and reinforcements were despatched from that place. The vanguard was attacked on the left flank eight miles from Wonderfontein, but beat off the enemy. On their return the troops were harassed by snipers. When they were about a mile and a half from the camp a trap was prepared for the Boers. Sine of the troops continued their march, while the others lay in wait for the enemy, who, however, noticed what was in store for them and disappeared. No casualties were reported on the British side. One Boer was killed and several wounded, one man subsequently dying of his wounds.

"The Heat of Summer" in South Africa.

Writing to his methor who lives at 8, Heaton-square, Warrington, Private J Abra,ha.ms, who is serving at the front with the 3rd South Lancashire Regiment says: —" Things are very quiet here now, and we are waiting for orders to come home. A few of our regiment were cut up not long since whilst: out on patrol and we lost one of the best officers in the regiment, He was found, riddled with bullets. We are having the heat of the suromn out here."

"Parliament, the Prelates and the Protestant Position."

A lecture with the above title was given in the Lecture-room at the Y.M.C.A, on Monday evening by the Rev Theodore A Howard, Vicar of St. Mat- thew's, Liverpool. The meeting, which was poorly attended, was presided over by Mr John Warburton who was supported by the Rev James Davies. The Lecturer, who was greeted with applause, referred briefly to the General Election. and said that, the question of lawlessness in the Cu arch had been for- gotten or ignored owing to the predominance of Imperial affairs. He then explained the two great Bills which would occupy the attention of Parlia- ment, viz.. The Church Discipline Bill, with five clauses (I) Maintenance of royal CI upremacy ; (2) Abolition of the Episcopal Veto; (3) A lay judge in the court of first instance; (4) Substitution of de- privation for imprisonment and (5) Simplification of procedure: That Bill would, he said, almost cer- tainly be introduced by one of the members for Liverpool. probably by Mr W McArthur. The other Bill would be brought in under the auspices of the Church Reform League and would be headed The Convocation Act, 1901. The object of the last- named would be to substitute sacerdotal supremacy for royal supremacy. The speaker went on to say that, on the authority of Mr Miller, secretary of the Church Assoeiation, 291 M.P.'s had been returned Ito Parliament who were either pledged to support 'the five points of the Church Discipline Bill,or who had expressed themselves in sympathy with the Protestant cause. After a reference to the "decisions " of the two archbishops, he went on to say that the Bishops of London, Bristol, Chester, Manchester and Rochester had given direct sanction to the use of incense and processional lights in church provided only that they were not used in the communion service. As examples, the Bishop, of London sanctioned tha ceremonial use of incense at, the introit (" Church Review," November 9th. 1899). The Rev W C Reid, vicar of .Coppenhall state. that," acting with the Bishop's advice," he proposed in future to use incense during the sing- ing of the hymn immediately preceding the service of the Holy Cemmuni (" Church Times," October, I3'h, 1899). The rev gentleman gave other examples of the use of incense and the use of other forms of service of the Roman Catholic church At the cenciusion of the lecture he moved the following resolution :—" That this meeting, believ- ing that the extirpation of Rornanism from the National Church is a matter of paramount import- ance not only to the Church but also to the nation at, lerge,aild seeing th st no effectual eteps had been taken by the Bench of Bishops as a whole to put an end to the gross scandal caused by Romish doctrines and practices in the Reformed Church of England— notably the mass and Sacramental Confession—calls upon the Government, to redeem the pledge made intthe House of Commons IS months since, and to take the matter vigorously in hand in the opening session of Parliament ; and, further, that the Secre- tary be requested to forward a copy of this resolu- tion to the Member of Parliament for the Division." —This was seconded and carried unanimously. Votes of thanks to the Speaker and the Chairman concluded the meeting.

THE EXAMINER' FRIDAY, DECEMBER 14 1900.

[ALL RIGFIIS RHSNRVED.f LIDENSING REFORM,

BY THE HON. SIDNEY PEEL.

allowed before the present holders of "grocers' lic3nses " should be required to give them up, and in allotting the new consolidated off-licenses, prefer- ence should be given to the old holders, if they so desired. CLUBS.

A good deal of attention has been directed to the sale of intoxicants in clubs. There has been a large development of the club movement during the last 15 years, especially of working-men's clubs. There are now altogether between 4,000 and 5,000 clubs in the United Kingdom, in which intoxicants are supplied. The towns having the greatest numbers (in 1897) were London 660, Manchester 85, Bradford Si, Oldham 69, Leeds 61, Dublin 51, Liverpool 49, Sheffield 41, Huddersfield 39, Stockport 34, Halifax 31, Salford 30, and Cardiff 29. ,There are some great associations of clubs, especially the Working Men's club and Institute Union (chiefly in London), and the Conservative Union, which exercise some control over and give advice to their affiliated members. These clubs are outside the licensing laws; a propri tary club, i.e., one in which the profits in the sale of liquor go to an individual or any group of individuals other than the club irself as a whole, is of course in the same position as any other house in which intoxicating liquors are sold without a license, and liable to all the penalties of the law. But in a properly constituted members' club, where the profits go to the club as a whole, it has been held that the sale of intoxicants to mem- bers is merely a distribution to them of what is already their own property, and that therefore there is no sale in the legal sense, so that the licensinz. laws with the restrictions they carry do not apply In some quarters, not. least among the publicans who fear to see their regular customers attracted elsewhere, a great deal of alarm is felt abut these clubs. It is said that their formation is simply due to the &sire to eva Is the restrictions of the licens- ing laws. ano that in Cime rif any further restrictions or a dinunution in the number of licensed houses, there would be a great outburst of uncontrolled drinking in clubs. To give colour to this theory, there are or have been about a dozea instances of public houses which have lost their licenses, being turned into clubs of a very disorderly character, and there are other in- stances, somewhat more numerous, in which brewers have startel clubs, or supplied the funds for so doing on condition that the club shall be tied to them. That this is a danzer is rec)guised by the Working 14.1 en's Club and Institute Union, whin re- fuses the privileges of affiliation to a club under any such obligation. _

Unhappily too,thera is no doubt that many Work- ing Men's Clubs ar3 not well conductel, but are the scenes of a great deal of unnecessary drinking ; Clubs, that is, which are properly constituted naem- bers' clubs, are not in anysense bogus clubs. Bogus clubs are of course entirely illegal, and the present powers of the police in dealing with places used for illicit retail sales would appear to be quite suffi- ,,ient for their suppression, it actively employed. But badly corlducte i though legitimate clubs they have no power to touch.

Just as railway carriages were bait originally on exactly the same pan as the old coaches, so work- ing men's clubs too often are cast naturally in the mould of the public-house, and old associations are not easily cut off. Men who used to drink in public-houses do not lose their habits suddenly when c•hey join a club; many clubs depend too much on the profits which they derive from the sale of intoxicants, and not upon subscriptions. But even at the worst a club is not a public-heuse; drinking will not remain the sole object of its existence, even if it is so at first; sooner or later public opinion will spring up to enforce better objects; club men do not like the reproach that their club is a mere tippling shop. Whatever may happen in the future, the growth of clubs cannot be attributed to a mere desire to evade the laws. It is true that in Cardiff a great number of drinking clubs did spring up after the Sunday Closing Act, but these ware reluced to 29 in 1897, and since then about half of these have been ctosed. In London, where clubs are very numerous, there can scarcely be said to be a diffi- culty in ooteining liquor ou;sile them. In Dublin clubs were said to have been forme I in anticioetion of Sunday closing, but i's the rest of Ireland there are very few clubs indeed. The real fact is that the extension of the franchise, spread of education, the general improvement of conditions among the working clas,es, and political influences heve had the greatest effeets in promoting clubs. Clubs have done and will do much to meet the needs awakened by all these things for better means of social inter- course and reureatio a than those provided by the public-house. The saying that the public-house is the poor man's club is not so true as it used to be

No better support could be rii-v-e-t-C-to-th-e-Cli-lb movement, than the suppression of or prevention of the mere drinking club: no one recognises more clearly than the clubmen thetiselves, and some legislation is desirable with this object. Such legis- lation must embrace all clubstit must prevent bogus clubs, and check disorder in legally constituted clubs, without putting any difficulties in the way of genuine and well-conducted clubs. We will sketch the oatlines of the scheme con- tained in the Minority Report of the Royal Com- mission, which follows pretty closel3 thai, recom- mended by the representatives of the Working Men's Club and Institute Union.

All clubs selling intoxicants should be registered and such sale should only be considered' legal with- out a license in properly registered clubs. Regi‹- tration should only be allowed when certain condi- tions are fulfilled, eg. the club must be a members' club of not loss than 25 members, with its property vested in the club as a whole. It must be under no obligation to any dealer in liquor, and must. n3t be on premises which have been used as a licensed house within the last five years. All members must be elected by the whole club, or its com- mittee, after an interval of at least a week between nomination and election; the rules must provide for the regular meetings of the committee, the cir- cumstances under which membership lapses, the amount of subscriptions, hours of opening and clos- ing, ant the objects of the club. The committee must be antlividually resp misible for the conduct of the club, and mast make sworn afil lavit that the nece-sary conditions are fulfilled. Distribution of intoxicants for consumption off the premises should be regarded as a legal sale, and no member under , 18 should be admitted.

Objection to registration shall only be allowel on the ground that the conditions are not fulfilled, or that the cimmittee are not persons of good charac- ter. If objection is made, the case shall be heard before the Recorder. or a County Court Judge, Registration shall be annually renewed, and it shall be regarded as a matter of course providei the necessary affidavits are made, and accounts pro- perly rendered, unless objection is made on one of four grounds.

(a) That the club is a disorderly house. (b) That it is u.ed merely for drinking purposes (c) That its frequenters are habitually drunk. . _ _ _ . _

(d) That the rules are habitually broken. On proof of ‘uch objections the 98,132,3 court as before should have power to inflict a fine or to can- cel registration. The court sha'l also have plwer, in case of a su4pect«,3 club, to order a special officer to inspect it and ex tmine its books. &se such a scheme, CUil&iil wic.h viizilanCe on the part of the police and their ptesent powsn, will suffice to put 10M1 hOr4,li or unle4irsblti clubs. It will not int.;rf-.ro with rIL-t es4entia feature of clubs namely thst thsv are orivate and not public insti- tutions, and iartake of tie character of a private house.

Hours of Closing on Week-Days.

More important parhaps than th question of Sunday closing is that of earlier closing on week- days, especially on Saturday nights. The figures quoted from Liverpool and Efl.nchester shew that 35 per cent. of arrests for drimkennhss take place on Saturdays, and most of this in the evening. It is generally acknowledged that drunkenness is greatest during the last hour or two of the day.and the " fierce drinking " takes place usually between 10 and 11 prn Many people, however, doubt the effect of earlier closing; it is a point that can only be settled by p.-qetipal x,arinient To do his the licensing authority should •given plwers to make orders for earlier closing by any time up to two hours,these orders to be annually renewable Such a plan would be eminently suitable to the varying conditions of different localities In Scotland a Isimilar discretionary power of earlier closing has been in existence (except in the 7 large towns) since 1887. It is a remarkable fact that it has been put into effect in every licensing district, with unani- mous testimony to its good result; indeed the large towns which are excluded from the operation of the rule, are constantly agitating to be included. At the same time there is no reason why intoxi- cants should be sold before 8 in the morning. Coffee-stalls and the like could as easily and more wholesomely supply any early morning needs 8 a.m. is the opening hour in Scotland, 'and there:is no reason to think that the English working man requires alcohol earlier than the Scot‘ish. Further the licensing authority should have power to close on electimidays, which are often the c ause of much drinking

===== 0002601_19001214_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
OTEUE BONA FIDE TRAVELLER.

There is one exception to the stringency of clos- ing regulations, that in favour of the bon fide traveller, which has been greatly abused. To be a bona fide traveller a man must have travelled at least three miles from where he lodged the precious night; this does not necessarily constitute him a bona fide traveller, but it is almost universally held to do so. It consequently results that some place may be overrun by a most undesirablo class of visitor, who drinks to his heart's content, while its own inhabitants are robbed of all peace and quiet without a remedy, and calm° even drink themselves. Outside the large towns the grievance is particularly felt. Almost everywhere the so- called bona fide traveller is a fraud and a nuisance, and ha 9 no other objeo. than mere tippling, a parti- cularly noxious form of drinking, whichl de- serves no consideration. Still there are some bona fide travellers, especially cyclists, and some allow- ance might be made for them. Licenses for hotels, that is houses with a certain sleeping accommoda- tion for strangers and no public bar, apart from the public house license, are desirable on other grounds as well, and these with restaurants should alone possess the privilege of serving travellers. Travellers should be defined as persons who have travelled at least seven miles from their last night's place of lodging, who are about to take a meal in the house, or who are about to 10(4;4 therein. To guard further against any abuse, the licensing authority should have the power of withdrawing the privilege, if they thought fit The licensin authority might also be given power to fix certain hours during which bona fide travellers might be served.

===== 0002601_19001214_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
SALE TO CHILDIVRN.

At present the law is that no spirits may be sold to children under the age of 16, and no kind of in- toxicating liquors to children under 13. But the fact that children may buy liquor and carry it away, whether as messengers or not, renders this law largely nugatory. Whatever view be taken of the necessity of alco- hol to an adult, no one can say that it is good for children, and few would. assert that i is well to familiarise children with the sights, sounds and smells of a public-house from an early age The prac ice of sending children for liqulr is bad for the children, and bad for the sender. Lazy and drunken parents are enabled to stay at home and go on drinking as long as money holds out, and cases of cruelty to children often arise from this cause ; children are also kept from school for the same reason. It is argued on the other side that it would be a hardship for many working-men, if their children were not allowed to be sent for their beer, and that if a man went himself, he would be likely to stay and take too much The hardship is often exaggerated, and if any were inflicted, it would be an inconvenience small in proportion to the benefit of the children. Many benches have now adopted ' the practice of advising licensed victuallers and others not to serve children under a certain age. The simplest and best plan is o forbid all sale of I intoxicants •o any child under 16, and to increase I the penalties imposed for knowingly serving chil- dren under age, which are now only 20s for the first, and 40s for the second offence.

THE COMBINATION OF THE TRADE IN INTOXICATING LIQUORS WITH OTHER TRADES, OCCUPATIONS, &C.

At the present day in Ireland much harm results from public-house licenses being held by persons dealing in all sorts of other goods, even clothes, and boots and shoes. Temptation to drink is always present, and a purchase is often sealed with too deep potations In England the separation of the trade in intoxicants from those in other goods, ex- cept of course eatables to be consumed on the pre- mises, is a principle recognised in practice, though many an unnecessary license is still attached in country districts to farms, mills and the like; and, while in some places like Cardiff bye-laws under the ferchant Shipping Act, 1894, forbid persons keep- ing lodging-houses for seamen and fishermen, to hold licenses for the sale of intoxicants, in other seaports a good deal of scandal and abuse is caused by this combination. The separation of the trades is not. howeve-, in law or practice regarded as essential in the case of off-licenses, there is a widespread feeling against what are known as grocers' licenses. In law there is no such thing as'a grocer's license, but there are four retail off-licenses for selling spirits, wine or sweets, which must be held by a shopkeeper, and three retail beer off-licenses and the cider license which may be so hell. In prac- tice the spirit, wine and beer licenses are very fre- quently held by grocers. and hence the name The wine and spirit licenses only permit sale in bottles, but this restriction does not apply to beer. Various complaints are made against these licenses. It is urged that the combination makes supervision difficult and the detection of con- sumption on the premises nearly impossible. It also gives facilities for circulating beer and spirits in carts and vans, so that intoxicants may be hawked about the country in contravention of the law. This kind of complaint is very common in Scotland, :but is also not unknown in, country districts in England. It is also urged that grocers' licenses unite both ' temptation and secrecy in urging women to drink, and that the grocers sometimes co-operate in the deception of husbands, or other responsible bill- payers, by entering intoxicants thus sold under the name of other goods. Detection of such offences is most difficult Police statistics cannot prove the drinking that results, as by its nature it is secret. All parties in the trans- action are strongly concerned in keeping it a secret, and, even if it is discovered, the discoverer, who is probably a friend or relative, has also strong reasons against publishing his knowledge. Under these circumstances it is surprising how many well authenticated cases of the practice there are. A remarkable case came from Chichester. There the workhouse officials were allowed a certain amount of groceries to be supplied in turn by a rotation of grocers in the town. Grocer after grocer supplied intoxieants instead and booked them as" groceries," till one grocer, more honour- able than the rest, came forward and divulged the practice That some grocers do indulge in these objection- able practices may be regarded as an established fact, but the real objection to the combination of the trades lies in the temp ation held out to women purchasing ordinary goods. Wi- hout any decep- tion or malpractice, but with perfec- secrecy, it is possible for a woman to purchase bottle after bottle of spirits with the deplorable results well known in many unhappy families. It is far safer to confine the trade in so danger- ous an article as intoxicating liquor to run entirely within its own channels, and not allow it to be combined with others The excise off-licenses should be consolidated, and no new ones granted except under the full discretion of the licensing authority, and to premises exclusively used for the sale of intoxicants. A period of 5 years might be
