NEWTONT CONGREGATIONAL Caunca—SALE OE WORK.

On Thursday afternoon last week Colonel W W Pilkington, VU., of "The Hazels," PrPse.ot, opened a sale of work in connection with the above church. The object in view was the raising of a sum of money—it is hoped £loo—towarris clearing off the debt incurred by the renovation and partial re-buildin2 of the schoolroom, which has cost £1,200. The sale was held in this handsome school- room,which a large number of willing workers had converted into a most delightful apartment.— The sale was continued on Saturday afternoon, when the Rev James Ryder, Vicar of Newton, was present. The Rev James Allatt said they did not propose to have any formal opening, but he alluded to the good feeling shown by Mr Ryder in being present at a Nonconformist gathering. There was a good attendance during the afternoon, and the sale was carried on briskly. The total receipts for the two days was £95, which were much in PXCORS of the anticipations of those who had the affair in hand.

EARLESTOWN 84 NEWTON

P.S A.

_ The usual weekly meeting of the Earlestown and District P.S.A.. Society was hell in the Cc-operative Hall on Sanday afternoon, when there was a good muster. Mr Markland presided, and an interesting address was given by Mr Plant the Haydock Missionary. Vocal items were given by Miss Watkins and recitations by Miss Huffar,whilst Miss Profit presided at the piano. FRYE Cinntcri COUNCIL, EARLESTOWN.

A gathering in connection with the Free Church Council of the Earlostown and Newton district was held in the Lecture Hall of the Wesleyan Chapel, Earlestown on Monday evening. Before the busi- ness proceedings tea was served, and a good num- ber partook thereof. Afterwards the chair was occupied by the Rev F E \filler, and he was sup- ported by the Revs J Allatt, C E Dove, E C Bocock, Mr G Dring,and Mr Wallace of Burnley. It appears it is the intention of the Council to hold a ten days' mission, commencing on the 17th of February, to be conducted by Mr Wallace, and the object of the gathering was to welcome that gentleman. The Rev E Miller introduced Mr Wallace to the meeting and the latter gave a very interesting address, ex- plaining his views and stating what he required doing in order to help the mission, and some novel ideas for publicly announcing the mission were expounded.

MILITA.RY TOURNAMENT -THE POLICE WIN THE CHALLENGE CUP.

A grand gymnastic display and military tournament in aid of the St Helens Young Men's Christian I.ssocia- tion and Gymnasium, was held in the Gymnasium, Duke-street, on Tuesday, by members of the Gymnasium and Borough Police force, with detach- ments of the Lancashire Huseari and Volunteers. The chief event of the evening, was the tug of war contest for the Silver Ch sllenge Cup, for which four teams (six aside) competed—namely, the Borough Police Force, Lancashire Huisars, -End VR. South Lancashire Regiment, and the 2od Lancashire Engineers. Considerable interest wIR attached to the contest, and in the first round the Police (with Sergeant H Hudson as captain) beat the Eir,rineers (under Sergeant-Inahlictor Seay,er) by 2 pulls to 1. In the second round the Volun- teers beat the Lancashire Hussars by 2 clear pulls. In the final the Police had the best oF the argument, and won the cup with 2 clear pulls. Colonel Murray then presented the cup to Sergeant Hudson, on behalf of the winners.

THE MORALITY oF EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON. Ow A PURIST.]

" Womanhood" Saeh was the title of a very pithy and plain spoken paper read by Miss Allatt of Newton before the members of the Women's Liberal Associetion on Monday evening in the Primi- tive Methodist S;hoolrootn Earlestown. Extracts from the paper are reprinted in another column, in the shape of a report of the gathering and doubt- less they will be fund wholesome reading, and of much valuable help to those who have tie welfare of wemen at heart. Very mach is contained in that one word " Wom-Inhood," hut how little is thought of it in Eerlestown. Passages in !Miss Allatt's paper, well-meaning enough, were doubtless written for the information only of the women to whom she intended reading it, and for that reason, certain passages have been deleted from the report of the mewing. and partly because of the plain language used. Possibly Miss Allatt has good grounds for bringing a sweeping charge of immodesty ngainst the young worneu of Narlestown and Newton. and perhaps here the sense of deceuey has been outraged by the statements which have been evi- dently male to her. Her attacks upon public works where fetnal 3 labour is employed were of the severest, especially as it related to the girls them- selves, and not to the management, If what, Miss Allatt stated is correct, it shows a condition of things, in our midst, of a most alarming character. We ,av "in our rni let," because Mies Allatt in her assertions, plainly alludes to the young women of Earlestown and Newton. Purists, were they ac- quainted with the extent of Miss Allatt'. chnrges, would cry shame, and yet the puriste are in Some measure responsible tor the condition of things diF- closed in tie paper. The intermixing of the sexea in publiciworks, also makes fr the degradation of the female, aud Miss Allatt points out that on the evi- dence of a man, toe language used by the young women and girls in the works she had in her mini was disgusting. Marriage is always stated ti be a sacred un lertaking, but it would perhaps not be wise to say what Miss Allatt maintains the girls think about marriage. It will be sufficient to say that, according to Miss Atilt', the girls think very little about the sacred side of mar...lege. All they have to do is to get married and they care little how they attain their end, P: may he said that I am not in agreement with Miss Allatt, but such a statement would not be correct. lam in perfect accord with Miss Allatt, all through her paper, and more especially in relation to the uplifting of tae moral et indent of women. "Some woman sell their womanhood cheaply "says the paper, and for an echo to this one has only to promenade some of the streets of E trlestown at night. True we have the Salvation Army doing its best, according to the lights of the members to uplift the masses, but then the salvation of youngwomen from themselves cannot be effected by the blare of a trumpet, and the thumping of a big drum on a public market place. We have the Manchester City Aission,whose work in the district is brimful of good,be even they do not deal with the evil referred to by Miss Allatt. Then there is the Free Church Council,in connection with which a mission is proposed to be held shortly and to this we look for some sort of assistance in dealing with the terrible evil lurking in our midst., To make a special effort and appeal during the MiF- I *don. and to lay bare in all its hideousness the crime, for it is a crime, of which our young girls are alleged to be guilty, would be of immeasurable benefit. Lastly we have the police,and their sphere, though not lying in the same direction as the re- ligious bodies mentioned, would be to root out and brush away the opportunities given for the cheapen- ing of womanhood. To particularise, one might draw the attention of the police to the condition of Ilaydock-street, any evening, but more especially on Friday and Saturday ',night, and here work in plenty can be found. If betting characters are found congregating in a public thoroughfare they are quickly moved on, but in flaylock-street, young men and women, aye and boys and girls can congregate to their heart's content, and indispu- table evidence can be given that the language and the acts are of a,moat disgusting character. Here- in lies the root of the cheapening of womanhood, but a clean sweep by the police would rob it of many opportunities. Miss Allatt's disclosure has come none too soon, and it can only he hoped thaa I the advice she gives will hoar the fruit it deserves.

THE ST. HELENS BOILER EXPLOSION.

Judgment of the Board of Trade inquiry. Severe Strictures on the Owner.

At St Helens Town Hall, on Wednesday morning. Mr. Howard Smith and Mr M'lntyre, the commissioners appointed by the Beard of Trade to inquire into the boiler explosion which took place at Sutton Heath Pot- tery, Mill-lane, Sutton, on November 8, gave judgment. Mr Howard Smith reviewed the whole of the evidence relating to the boiler, which was bought second-hand by Mr Twist, on January 7, 1892, and was afterwards allowed to lie out exposed to the weather for four years. and then fixed up in ordinary bricks and mortar at the Sutton Heath Pottery. The boiler plates after tha explosion were found to vary from a quarter of an inch down to the thickoess of paper. The commissioners found the ex- plosion was caused by over-pressure of steam, a portion of the shell plate, where, in contact with the brickwork, having become so waged by corrosion, owing to damp, as to be unable to withstand any useful pressure. The boiler was not properly s‘i.c, up or examined. Mr. Twist was told about two months ;Jac,. the explosion that a portion of the plates was very thin, and he was likely - to have trouble with it, but ho tailea to take proper measures to eosure its being avorked under safe conditions. The court found Mr. 'Ft:P.lst very seriously to blame for the explosion. He had a large family, and his property was encum- bered. Mr. Howard Smith said the Court always con- sidered the circumstances of those it had to deal with. At the same time, Mr. Twist had been guilty of such gross carelessness that they would be justified in order- ing him to pay ths full costs. Instead of the whole £llO, they would, under the circumstances, order him to pay £3O, and the Court wared the Blard of Trade to give him time to pay. Fortunately for Mr Twist, no one bad been killed,or.his liability might have been unend.ng.

EARLESTOWN AND DISTRICT WOMEN'S LIBERAL ASSOCIATION.

The monthly meeting of the mornbers of this a.ssocia- tiou was held in the Primitive Wthodist schoolroom on Monday afternoon. There was only a moderate atten- dance, but ths proceedings were of an interesting character.

The chief item of business was a paper read by Miss Allett, entitled" Womanhood" which was a subject, Miss Anent said,in which she always felt the deepest in- terest, seeing they were gathered together as a band of women, whose very presence at such meetings as those, and membership of such an association as theirs, in- dicated an intelligent desire to do all that women could do for the uplifting of their dear old country. She had sometimes been asked what was the use of the Women's Liberal Association and why she cared to belong to it, to which she invariably replied " becawe it gives one at least a small oppertunity of doing something for one's fellow women upon whom the unjust laws fall heavies:, and laws which will never be altered unless women de- termine that they shall be." Womanhood was a very great subject, too vast to be entered kite in one brief afternoon. First of all she wanted to speak upon their womanhood in relation to their country. Everyone present loved their country and wished to see it not only the greatest country in the world, but the purest and best. During the past few years, women had come greatly to the aid of their country, flehtine its battles as bravely as any soldier in the battlefield. They had only to consider for one minute what had been done, and how women like Mrs. Joseph Butler had faced the shot and shell of bitter sarcasm, and worse, of men, in order that womanhood might be treated with more jnstice. In the cause of temperance too, they knew that the quiet systematic r work of such societies as the British Women's Temperanee Association was having an influence for good in their land, for ifi the women could be won on to the side of sobriety, they all knew what the effect would be ; their homes would he better, their men would have less cause to be drunkards, and Enelend would begin to be free of its greatest chain of slavery. Then they thought of such associations as their own and the work being dote. True they had no Parliamentary votrebut their voice had been heard and their influence felt in Parliament, for by persistently bringing its claims before its members, rtnny had been compelled to take notice of the fact that women were wide awake, and meant to have wrongs righted, and , especially where it touched their fellow-women. Many people said that women's work lay in their own homes, and that they should not bother themselves about politi- cal work, and she felt she could not do better, in relation to that than read a shoit extract frt m Ruskin. He said —" Generally we are under an impression that a man's duties are public, and a woman's private, but this is not altogethFr so. A man his a personal work or dnty relating to his own home, and a public work or duty which is the expansion of the other relating to the State. So a woman has a personal work or duty, which is also the expansion of that. Now a man's work for his own home is/ to secure its maintenance, progress, and defence ; the woman's to secure its order, comfort, and loveliness. Expand both these functions, The mans' duty as a member of the Commonwealth is to assist in the maintenance, in the advance, in the defence of the State. The women's duty as a member of the Commonwealth is to assist in the ordering, in the comforting, and in the beautiful adorr- ment of the State. What the man is at his own gate. defending it if need be sgainst insult an) spoil, that also not in a less, but in a more devoted measure he is to be at the gate of his country, leaving his home, if need be even ta the spoiler, to do his more incumbent work there And in like manner, what the woman is to be withia her gates as a centre of order, the balm of distress, and the mirror of beauty, that she is aico to be without the gates where order is more difficult, distress more immi- nent, loveliness more rare." To her mind they had there the:whole thing put clearly before theinnt was a duty for women to use the very best of their womanhood not only in the four walls of their home, but outside it also. In working for the genera! interests of their land, their personality was to a great extent lost sight of, but than could not be said in relation to their worn in their own district. They were known amongst their neighbours, and what they were in their own homes came out in various ware in spite of themselves ; they all had a cer tam n amount of personality ateut them. No one could deny that the most seer d and be iutiful side of womanhood was to be felled in motherhood. There wan a danger, a growing danger in these days, of women making their womanhood cheap. All of them might and ought to do something to remedy that. She wanted to make an appeal to the mothers preeen: to try with their ' girls at home to do something in that direction. Show them that so much lay in their power if they would but use their eromanhool rightly, teach them that it was given to them as the greatest gift they had, and to ' keep it pore, to use it for good, wou'd do much to better the world. (Applause). Lately there had crept into custom a more generel use tor the word " woman," and she thought the name was becoming more houourd in conseqeence. She honey nl the change had come about greatly on account of the word " lady" bsine abused. In society they knew there was great need for reform, and it was always refreshing and gratifTing to see and hear of women nobly striving to work on behalf of womanhood from that circle. But while they looked on and criticised or admired toy turn, do not let them for- get that they too lead meets to do in relation te the class to which they belongEd, and as those in the higher classes reacts a heloing hand to those less fortunate in life, so they too mieht do their share though it might seem a smeller thing. A good woman, wherever she was found, was a veritable green, she drew and attracted, though she might have little or no outward beauty. Her influence went out far and wide, though it would be greatest in her own home. A good woman left footprints behind her, the memory of her life upheld others in its struggles and she left a track ef lieht which showed others the upward path and the possibilities of a pare and holy womanhood, (Applause.) A cordial vote of thanks wan, accorded Miss Allatt, on the motion of Miss Marrow seconded by Mrs Jones.

AT CHRISTMAS TIME. You are puzzled what to give your friends and neighbours. You want to give them something good, something useful, some- thing that will be a pleasant reminder of your thoughtfulness. Now, there is no present more welcome in a household than a canister of really good tea. It does not spoil if not used immediately, and is never out of season. There is, too, a peculiar appropriateness in a present of tea being made at Christmas when each home has its festival. But when you buy, be sure you get good tea, tea that will make a friend of every lady who drinks it, a tea like Brooke, Bond's Tea, a small spoonful of which is stronger and better than a large spoonful of other tea. • SONS OF TEMPERANCE RUNCORN. The fortnightly meeting of this society was held on Saturday night in the Greenway-road school. The President Mr Thomas R Whyte, occupied the chair. Mr 6amuel II Tewprley oi Lostock, gave an encouraging address, in which ho congratulated the "division " on the progress they were making, Ho referred to the fact that five candidates had that, night been proposed for membership. Temperance men should be true to their principles in whats.ver company they were placed ; then their influence would be potent for good. The following officers also gave short. addresses :—Moisrs Thomas Cooke, Thomas G dolt, Thomas lieesom, Uaniel Bisbrown and the Chairman.

This match was played at Runcorn on Saturday, the Winmarleigh being without Doody and Byrant. Lociing the toss they pressed their opponents nearly all the first half, but no score ensued, the play of the Brunswick custodian being highly commendable. But towards the end of the second half in s4mi-darknee. Brunswick managed to place two goals to their credit, the game thus ending in a win for the latter by two to nil. COMBINATION. --- Warrington v. Wrexham. These teams played their return fixture on Wrexham Racecourse on Saturday. The weather Wag unfavour- able, a high wind prevailing. Tbere wa:i a wretched attendance. The home team bad the best of it, though the Warringtonians several times got within shooting range, but their essays in front of goal were very weak. The final rveult was—Wrexham. five goats ; Warrington, WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT LEAGUE. I Second Div;sion. Warrington St. Mary's v. Latchford Britannia. Played at Howley on Saturday. The opening stages were greatly in favour of the Saints, Albert Meachin and Howell being tee most Conspicuous in the attack. ' New ~1 opened the scoring for the visitors from a corner I Half-Arne : Britannia, one goal ; St Mary's, nil. In the second half Latchford were penned in their own quarter, Albert Meachin and Hawtrey having hard lines ,1 on several occasions. Tha visitors broke away and Sherratt further added to their score. A cross shot by Howell narrowly missed success in the last minute. Final: Britannia Combi•oation two goals, St Mary's nit. Stockton Heath v. Warrington Athletic. This League match was played at Stockton Heath on Saturday. From the start the Athletic forced their way to the homesters' quarters, and Fielding scored, after some exciting play. Soon after Kee7on put the second through, being followed by another by Fielding shortly afterwards. The visitors kept up the attack, and Kenyon broke away and scored, but it was ruled off- side. Half-lhue arrived, the visitors leading by three goals to nil. From the re-start the homesters rushed away, but were beaten back. Jackson ran away drown the field and missed a good chance to score. Towards the finish the Athletics had the best of matteri, the tacks showing good form and from a free kick the hrinesters' custodian saved splendidly.
